Due to the large size of our pre-training dataset, over-fitting is not a major concern and the details of training CLIP are simplified compared to the implementation of Zhang et al. (2020).

[P09-S00099] PDF pages [4]
We train CLIP from scratch without initializing the image encoder with ImageNet weights or the text encoder with pre-trained weights.

[P09-S00100] PDF pages [4]
We do not use the non-linear projection between the representation and the contrastive embedding space, a change which was introduced by Bachman et al. (2019) and popularized by Chen et al. (2020b).

[P09-S00101] PDF pages [4]
We instead use only a linear projection to map from each encoder's representation to the multi-modal embedding space.

[P09-S00102] PDF pages [4]
We did not notice a difference in training efficiency between the two versions and speculate that non-linear projections may be co-adapted with details of current image only in self-supervised representation learning methods.

[P09-S00103] PDF pages [4]
We also remove the text transformation function tu from Zhang et al. (2020) which samples a single sentence at uniform from the text since many of the (image, text) pairs in CLIP's pre-training dataset are only a single sentence.

[P09-S00104] PDF pages [4]
We also simplify the image transformation function tv.

[P09-S00105] PDF pages [4]
A random square crop from resized images is the only data augmentation used during training.

[P09-S00106] PDF pages [4]
Finally, the temperature parameter which controls the range of the logits in the softmax, τ, is directly optimized during training as a log-parameterized multiplicative scalar to avoid turning as a hyper-parameter.

[P09-S00107] PDF pages [4]
We consider two different architectures for the image encoder.

[P09-S00108] PDF pages [4]
For the first, we use ResNet-50 (He et al., 2016a) as the base architecture for the image encoder due to its widespread adoption and proven performance.

[P09-S00109] PDF pages [4]
We make several modifications to the original version using the ResNet- D improvements from He et al. (2019) and the antialiased rect-2 blur pooling from Zhang (2019).

[P09-S00110] PDF pages [4]
We also replace the global average pooling layer with an attention pooling mechanism.

[P09-S00111] PDF pages [4, 5]
The attention pooling is implemented as a single layer of “transformer-style” multi-head QKV attention where the query is conditioned on the global average-pooled Learning Transferable Visual Models From Natural Language Supervision 5 # image_encoder - ResNet or Vision Transformer # text_encoder - CBOW or Text Transformer # I[n, h, w, c] - minibatch of aligned images # T[n, l] - minibatch of aligned texts # W_i[d_i, d_e] - learned proj of image to embed # W_t[d_t, d_e] - learned proj of text to embed # t - learned temperature parameter # extract feature representations of each modality I_f = image_encoder(I) #[n, d_i] T_f = text_encoder(T) #[n, d_t] # joint multimodal embedding [n, d_e] I_e = l2_normalize(np.dot(I_f, W_i), axis=1) T_e = l2_normalize(np.dot(T_f, W_t), axis=1) # scaled pairwise cosine similarities [n, n] logits = np.dot(I_e, T_e.T) * np.exp(t) # symmetric loss function labels = np.arange(n) loss_i = cross_entropy_loss(logits, labels, axis=0) loss_t = cross_entropy_loss(logits, labels, axis=1) loss = (loss_i + loss_t)/2 Figure 3.

[P09-S00112] PDF pages [5]
For the second architecture, we experiment with the recently introduced Vision Transformer (ViT) (Dosovitskiy et al., 2020).

[P09-S00113] PDF pages [5]
We closely follow their implementation with only the minor modification of adding an additional layer normalization to the combined patch and position embeddings before the transformer and use a slightly different initialization scheme.

[P09-S00114] PDF pages [5]
The text encoder is a Transformer (Vaswani et al., 2017) with the architecture modifications described in Radford et al. (2019).

[P09-S00115] PDF pages [5]
As a base size we use a 63M-parameter 12- layer 512-wide model with 8 attention heads.

[P09-S00116] PDF pages [5]
The transformer operates on a lower-cased byte pair encoding (BPE) representation of the text with a 49,152 vocab size (Sennrich et al., 2015).

[P09-S00117] PDF pages [5]
For computational efficiency, the max sequence length was capped at 76.

[P09-S00118] PDF pages [5]
The text sequence is bracketed with [SOS] and [EOS] tokens and the activations of the highest layer of the transformer at the [EOS] token are treated as the feature representation of the text which is layer normalized and then linearly projected into the multi-modal embedding space.

[P09-S00119] PDF pages [5]
Masked self-attention was used in the text encoder to preserve the ability to initialize with a pre-trained language model or add language modeling as an auxiliary objective, though exploration of this is left as future work.

[P09-S00120] PDF pages [5]
While previous computer vision research has often scaled models by increasing the width (Mahajan et al., 2018) or depth (He et al., 2016a) in isolation, for the ResNet image encoders we adapt the approach of Tan & Le (2019) which found that allocating additional compute across all of width, depth, and resolution outperforms only allocating it to only one dimension of the model.

[P09-S00121] PDF pages [5]
While Tan & Le (2019) tune the ratio of compute allocated to each dimension for their EfficientNet architecture, we use a simple baseline of allocating additional compute equally to increasing the width, depth, and resolution of the model.

[P09-S00122] PDF pages [5]
For the text encoder, we only scale the width of the model to be proportional to the calculated increase in width of the ResNet and do not scale the depth at all, as we found CLIP's performance to be less sensitive to the capacity of the text encoder.

[P09-S00123] PDF pages [5]
We train a series of 5 ResNets and 3 Vision Transformers.

[P09-S00124] PDF pages [5]
For the ResNets we train a ResNet-50, a ResNet-101, and then 3 more which follow EfficientNet-style model scaling and use approximately 4x, 16x, and 64x the compute of a ResNet-50.

[P09-S00125] PDF pages [5]
They are denoted as RN50x4, RN50x16, and RN50x64 respectively.

[P09-S00126] PDF pages [5]
For the Vision Transformers we train a ViT-B/32, a ViT-B/16, and a ViT-L/14.

[P09-S00127] PDF pages [5]
We train all models for 32 epochs.

[P09-S00128] PDF pages [5]
We use the Adam optimizer (Kingma & Ba, 2014) with decoupled weight decay regularization (Loshchilov & Hutter, 2017) applied to all weights that are not gains or biases, and decay the learning rate using a cosine schedule (Loshchilov & Hutter, 2016).

[P09-S00129] PDF pages [5]
Initial hyperparameters were set using a combination of grid searches, random search, and manual tuning on the baseline ResNet- 50 model when trained for 1 epoch.

[P09-S00130] PDF pages [5]
Hyper-parameters were then adapted heuristically for larger models due to computational constraints.

[P09-S00131] PDF pages [5]
The learnable temperature parameter τ was initialized to the equivalent of 0.07 from (Wu et al., 2018) and clipped to prevent scaling the logits by more than 100 which we found necessary to prevent training in-stability.

[P09-S00132] PDF pages [5]
We use a very large minibatch size of 32,768.

[P09-S00133] PDF pages [5]
Mixed-precision (Micikevicius et al., 2017) was used to accelerate training and save memory.

[P09-S00134] PDF pages [5]
To save additional memory, gradient checkpointing (Griewank & Walther, 2000; Chen et al., 2016), half-precision Adam statistics (Dhariwal et al., 2020), and half-precision stochastically rounded text encoder weights were used.

[P09-S00135] PDF pages [5]
The calculation of embedding similarities was also sharded with individual GPUs computing only the subset of the pairwise similarities necessary for their local batch of embeddings.

[P09-S00136] PDF pages [5]
The largest ResNet model, RN50x64, took 18 days to train on 592 V100 GPUs while the largest Vision Transformer took 12 days on 256 V100 GPUs.

[P09-S00137] PDF pages [5]
For the ViT-L/14 we also pre-train at a higher 336 pixel resolution for one additional epoch to boost performance similar to FixRes (Touvron et al., 2019).

[P09-S00138] PDF pages [5]
We denote this model as ViT-L/14@336px.

[P09-S00139] PDF pages [5]
Unless otherwise specified, all results reported in this paper as “CLIP” use this model which we found to perform best.

[P09-S00140] PDF pages [6]
In computer vision, zero-shot learning usually refers to the study of generalizing to unseen object categories in image classification (Lampert et al., 2009).

[P09-S00141] PDF pages [6]
We instead use the term in a broader sense and study generalization to unseen datasets.

[P09-S00142] PDF pages [6]
We motivate this as a proxy for performing unseen tasks, as aspired to in the zero-data learning paper of Larochelle et al. (2008).

[P09-S00143] PDF pages [6]
While much research in the field of unsupervised learning focuses on the representation learning capabilities of machine learning systems, we motivate studying zero-shot transfer as a way of measuring the task-learning capabilities of machine learning systems.

[P09-S00144] PDF pages [6]
In this view, a dataset evaluates performance on a task on a specific distribution.

[P09-S00145] PDF pages [6]
However, many popular computer vision datasets were created by the research community primarily as benchmarks to guide the development of generic image classification methods rather than measuring performance on a specific task.

[P09-S00146] PDF pages [6]
While it is reasonable to say that the SVHN dataset measures the task of street number transcription on the distribution of Google Street View photos, it is unclear what “real” task the CIFAR-10 dataset measures.

[P09-S00147] PDF pages [6]
It is clear, however, what distribution CIFAR-10 is drawn from - TinyImages (Torralba et al., 2008).

[P09-S00148] PDF pages [6]
On these kinds of datasets, zero-shot transfer is more an evaluation of CLIP's robustness to distribution shift and domain generalization rather than task generalization.

[P09-S00149] PDF pages [6]
Please see Section 3.3 for analysis focused on this.

[P09-S00150] PDF pages [6]
To our knowledge, Visual N-Grams (Li et al., 2017) first studied zero-shot transfer to existing image classification datasets in the manner described above.

[P09-S00151] PDF pages [6]
It is also the only other work we are aware of that has studied zero-shot transfer to standard image classification datasets using a generically pre-trained model and serves as the best reference point for contextualizing CLIP.

[P09-S00152] PDF pages [6]
Their approach learns the parameters of a dictionary of 142,806 visual n-grams (spanning 1- to 5- grams) and optimizes these n-grams using a differential version of Jelinek-Mercer smoothing to maximize the probability of all text n-grams for a given image.

[P09-S00153] PDF pages [6]
In order to perform zero-shot transfer, they first convert the text of each of the dataset's class names into its n-gram representation and then compute its probability according to their model, predicting the one with the highest score.

[P09-S00154] PDF pages [6]
Our focus on studying zero-shot transfer as an evaluation of task learning is inspired by work demonstrating task learning in the field of NLP.

[P09-S00155] PDF pages [6]
To our knowledge Liu et al. (2018) first identified task learning as an “unexpected side-effect” when a language model trained to generate Wikipedia articles learned to reliably transliterate names between languages.

[P09-S00156] PDF pages [6]
While GPT-1 (Radford et al., 2018) focused on pre- training as a transfer learning method to improve supervised fine-tuning, it also included an ablation study demonstrating that the performance of four heuristic zero-shot transfer methods improved steadily over the course of pre-training, without any supervised adaption.

[P09-S00157] PDF pages [6]
This analysis served as the basis for GPT-2 (Radford et al., 2019) which focused exclusively on studying the task-learning capabilities of language models via zero-shot transfer.

[P09-S00158] PDF pages [6]
CLIP is pre-trained to predict if an image and a text snippet are paired together in its dataset.

[P09-S00159] PDF pages [6]
To perform zero-shot classification, we reuse this capability.

[P09-S00160] PDF pages [6]
For each dataset, we use the names of all the classes in the dataset as the set of potential text pairings and predict the most probable (image, text) pair according to CLIP.

[P09-S00161] PDF pages [6]
In a bit more detail, we first compute the feature embedding of the image and the feature embedding of the set of possible texts by their respective encoders.

[P09-S00162] PDF pages [6]
The cosine similarity of these embeddings is then calculated, scaled by a temperature parameter τ, and normalized into a probability distribution via a softmax.

[P09-S00163] PDF pages [6]
Note that this prediction layer is a multinomial logistic regression classifier with L2-normalized inputs, L2-normalized weights, no bias, and temperature scaling.

[P09-S00164] PDF pages [6]
When interpreted this way, the image encoder is the computer vision backbone which computes a feature representation for the image and the text encoder is a hypernetwork (Ha et al., 2016) which generates the weights of a linear classifier based on the text specifying the visual concepts that the classes represent.

[P09-S00165] PDF pages [6]
Lei Ba et al. (2015) first introduced a zero-shot image classifier of this form while the idea of generating a classifier from natural language dates back to at least Elhoseiny et al. (2013).

[P09-S00166] PDF pages [6]
Continuing with this interpretation, every step of CLIP pre-training can be viewed as optimizing the performance of a randomly created proxy to a computer vision dataset which contains 1 example per class and has 32,768 total classes defined via natural language descriptions.

[P09-S00167] PDF pages [6]
For zero-shot evaluation, we cache the zero-shot classifier once it has been computed by the text encoder and reuse it for all subsequent predictions.

[P09-S00168] PDF pages [6]
This allows the cost of generating it to be amortized across all the predictions in a dataset.

[P09-S00169] PDF pages [6]
In Table 1 we compare Visual N-Grams to CLIP.

[P09-S00170] PDF pages [6]
The best CLIP model improves accuracy on ImageNet from a proof of concept 11.5% to 76.2% and matches the performance of the original ResNet-50 despite using none of the 1.28 million crowd-labeled training examples available for this dataset.

[P09-S00171] PDF pages [6]
Additionally, the top-5 accuracy of CLIP models are noticeably higher than their top-1, and this model has a 95% top-5 accuracy, matching Inception-V4 (Szegedy et al., 2016).

[P09-S00172] PDF pages [7]
Comparing CLIP to prior zero-shot transfer image classi- fication results.

[P09-S00173] PDF pages [7]
CLIP improves performance on all three datasets by a large amount.

[P09-S00174] PDF pages [7]
This improvement reflects many differences in the 4 years since the development of Visual N-Grams (Li et al., 2017).

[P09-S00175] PDF pages [7]
CLIP is a significant step towards flexible and practical zero-shot computer vision classifiers.

[P09-S00176] PDF pages [7]
As mentioned above, the comparison to Visual N-Grams is meant for contextualizing the performance of CLIP and should not be interpreted as a direct methods comparison between CLIP and Visual N-Grams as many performance relevant differences between the two systems were not controlled for.

[P09-S00177] PDF pages [7]
For in-stance, we train on a dataset that is 10x larger, use a vision model that requires nearly 100x more compute per prediction, likely used over 1000x their training compute, and use a transformer-based model which did not exist when Visual N-Grams was published.

[P09-S00178] PDF pages [7]
As a closer comparison, we trained a CLIP ResNet-50 on the same YFCC100M dataset that Visual N-Grams was trained on and found it matched their reported ImageNet performance within a V100 GPU day.

[P09-S00179] PDF pages [7]
This baseline was also trained from scratch instead of being initialized from pre-trained ImageNet weights as in Visual N-Grams.

[P09-S00180] PDF pages [7]
CLIP also outperforms Visual N-Grams on the other 2 re-ported datasets.

[P09-S00181] PDF pages [7]
On aYahoo, CLIP achieves a 95% reduction in the number of errors, and on SUN, CLIP more than doubles the accuracy of Visual N-Grams.

[P09-S00182] PDF pages [7]
To conduct a more comprehensive analysis and stress test, we implement a much larger evaluation suite detailed in Appendix A.

[P09-S00183] PDF pages [7]
In total we expand from the 3 datasets reported in Visual N- Grams to include over 30 datasets and compare to over 50 existing computer vision systems to contextualize results.

[P09-S00184] PDF pages [7]
Most standard image classification datasets treat the information naming or describing classes which enables natural language based zero-shot transfer as an afterthought.

[P09-S00185] PDF pages [7]
The vast majority of datasets annotate images with just a numeric id of the label and contain a file mapping these ids back to their names in English.

[P09-S00186] PDF pages [7]
Some datasets, such as Flowers102 and GTSRB, don't appear to include this mapping at all in their released versions preventing zero-shot transfer entirely.2 For many datasets, we observed these labels may be 2Alec learned much more about flower species and German traffic signs over the course of this project than he originally anticipated.

[P09-S00187] PDF pages [7]
Prompt engineering and ensembling improve zero- shot performance.

[P09-S00188] PDF pages [7]
Compared to the baseline of using contextless class names, prompt engineering and ensembling boost zero-shot classification performance by almost 5 points on average across 36 datasets.

[P09-S00189] PDF pages [7]
This improvement is similar to the gain from using 4 times more compute with the baseline zero-shot method but is “free” when amortized over many predictions.

[P09-S00190] PDF pages [7]
When the name of a class is the only information provided to CLIP's text encoder it is unable to differentiate which word sense is meant due to the lack of context.

[P09-S00191] PDF pages [7]
In some cases multiple meanings of the same word might be included as different classes in the same dataset!

[P09-S00192] PDF pages [7]
This happens in ImageNet which contains both construction cranes and cranes that fly.

[P09-S00193] PDF pages [7]
Another example is found in classes of the Oxford-IIIT Pet dataset where the word boxer is, from context, clearly referring to a breed of dog, but to a text encoder lacking context could just as likely refer to a type of athlete.

[P09-S00194] PDF pages [7]
Another issue we encountered is that it's relatively rare in our pre-training dataset for the text paired with the image to be just a single word.

[P09-S00195] PDF pages [7]
Usually the text is a full sentence describing the image in some way.

[P09-S00196] PDF pages [7]
This often improves performance over the baseline of using only the label text.

[P09-S00197] PDF pages [7]
For instance, just using this prompt improves accuracy on ImageNet by 1.3%.

[P09-S00198] PDF pages [8]
Learning Transferable Visual Models From Natural Language Supervision 8 Similar to the “prompt engineering” discussion around GPT- 3 (Brown et al., 2020; Gao et al., 2020), we have also observed that zero-shot performance can be significantly improved by customizing the prompt text to each task.

[P09-S00199] PDF pages [8]
A few, non exhaustive, examples follow.

[P09-S00200] PDF pages [8]
We found on several fine-grained image classification datasets that it helped to specify the category.

[P09-S00201] PDF pages [8]
Likewise, on Food101 specifying a type of food and on FGVC Aircraft a type of aircraft helped too.

[P09-S00202] PDF pages [8]
For OCR datasets, we found that putting quotes around the text or number to be recognized improved performance.

[P09-S00203] PDF pages [8]
Finally, we found that on satellite image classification datasets it helped to specify that the images were of this form and we use variants of “a satellite photo of a {label}.

[P09-S00204] PDF pages [8]
We also experimented with ensembling over multiple zero-shot classifiers as another way of improving performance.

[P09-S00205] PDF pages [8]
These classifiers are computed by using different context prompts such as 'A photo of a big {label}” and “A photo of a small {label}”.

[P09-S00206] PDF pages [8]
We construct the ensemble over the embedding space instead of probability space.

[P09-S00207] PDF pages [8]
This allows us to cache a single set of averaged text embeddings so that the compute cost of the ensemble is the same as using a single classifier when amortized over many predictions.

[P09-S00208] PDF pages [8]
We've observed ensembling across many generated zero-shot classifiers to reliably improve performance and use it for the majority of datasets.

[P09-S00209] PDF pages [8]
On ImageNet, we ensemble 80 different context prompts and this improves performance by an additional 3.5% over the single default prompt discussed above.

[P09-S00210] PDF pages [8]
When considered together, prompt engineering and ensembling improve ImageNet accuracy by almost 5%.

[P09-S00211] PDF pages [8]
In Figure 4 we visualize how prompt engineering and ensembling change the performance of a set of CLIP models compared to the contextless baseline approach of directly embedding the class name as done in Li et al. (2017).

[P09-S00212] PDF pages [8]
Since task-agnostic zero-shot classifiers for computer vision have been understudied, CLIP provides a promising opportunity to gain a better understanding of this type of model.

[P09-S00213] PDF pages [8]
In this section, we conduct a study of various properties of CLIP's zero-shot classifiers.

[P09-S00214] PDF pages [8]
As a first question, we look simply at how well zero-shot classifiers perform.

[P09-S00215] PDF pages [8]
To contextualize this, we compare to the performance of a simple off-the-shelf baseline: fitting a fully supervised, regularized, logistic regression classifier on the features of the canonical ResNet-50.

[P09-S00216] PDF pages [8]
In Figure 5 we show this comparison across 27 datasets.

[P09-S00217] PDF pages [8]
Please see Appendix A for details of datasets and setup.

[P09-S00218] PDF pages [8]
Zero-shot CLIP is competitive with a fully supervised baseline.

[P09-S00219] PDF pages [8]
Across a 27 dataset eval suite, a zero-shot CLIP classifier outperforms a fully supervised linear classifier fitted on ResNet-50 features on 16 datasets, including ImageNet.

[P09-S00220] PDF pages [8]
Looking at individual datasets reveals some interesting behavior.

[P09-S00221] PDF pages [8]
On fine-grained classification tasks, we observe a wide spread in performance.

[P09-S00222] PDF pages [8]
On two of these datasets, Stanford Cars and Food101, zero-shot CLIP outperforms logistic regression on ResNet-50 features by over 20% while on two others, Flowers102 and FGVCAircraft, zero-shot CLIP underperforms by over 10%.

[P09-S00223] PDF pages [8]
On OxfordPets and Birdsnap, performance is much closer.

[P09-S00224] PDF pages [8]
We suspect these difference are primarily due to varying amounts of per-task supervision between WIT and ImageNet.

[P09-S00225] PDF pages [8]
On “general” object classification datasets such as ImageNet, CIFAR10/100, STL10, and PascalVOC2007 performance is relatively similar with a slight advantage for zero-shot CLIP in all cases.

[P09-S00226] PDF pages [8]
On STL10, CLIP achieves 99.3% overall which appears to be a new state of the art despite not using any training examples.

[P09-S00227] PDF pages [8]
Zero-shot CLIP significantly outperforms a ResNet-50 on two datasets measuring action recognition in videos.

[P09-S00228] PDF pages [8]
On Kinetics700, CLIP outperforms a ResNet-50 by 14.5%.

[P09-S00229] PDF pages [8]
Zero-shot CLIP also outperforms a ResNet-50's features by 7.7% on UCF101.

[P09-S00230] PDF pages [8]
We speculate this is due to natural language providing wider supervision for visual concepts involving verbs, compared to the noun-centric object supervision in ImageNet.

[P09-S00231] PDF pages [9]
Zero-shot CLIP outperforms few-shot linear probes.

[P09-S00232] PDF pages [9]
Zero-shot CLIP matches the average performance of a 4-shot linear classifier trained on the same feature space and nearly matches the best results of a 16-shot linear classifier across publicly available models.

[P09-S00233] PDF pages [9]
For both BiT-M and SimCLRv2, the best performing model is highlighted.

[P09-S00234] PDF pages [9]
Light gray lines are other models in the eval suite.

[P09-S00235] PDF pages [9]
The 20 datasets with at least 16 examples per class were used in this analysis.

[P09-S00236] PDF pages [9]
These results highlight the poor capability of zero-shot CLIP on more complex tasks.

[P09-S00237] PDF pages [9]
By contrast, non-expert humans can robustly perform several of these tasks, such as counting, satellite image classification, and traffic sign recognition, suggesting significant room for improvement.

[P09-S00238] PDF pages [9]
However, we caution that it is unclear whether measuring zero-shot transfer, as opposed to few-shot transfer, is a meaningful evaluation for difficult tasks that a learner has no prior experience with, such as lymph node tumor classification for almost all humans (and possibly CLIP).

[P09-S00239] PDF pages [9]
While comparing zero-shot performance to fully supervised models contextualizes the task-learning capabilities of CLIP, comparing to few-shot methods is a more direct comparison, since zero-shot is its limit.

[P09-S00240] PDF pages [9]
In Figure 6, we visualize how zero-shot CLIP compares to few-shot logistic regression on the features of many image models including the best publicly available ImageNet models, self-supervised learning methods, and CLIP itself.

[P09-S00241] PDF pages [9]
While it is intuitive to expect zero-shot to underperform one-shot, we instead find that zero-shot CLIP matches the performance of 4-shot logistic regression on the same feature space.

[P09-S00242] PDF pages [9]
This is likely due to an important difference between the zero-shot and few-shot approach.

[P09-S00243] PDF pages [9]
First, CLIP's zero-shot classifier is generated via natural language which allows for visual concepts to be directly specified (“communicated”).

[P09-S00244] PDF pages [9]
By contrast, “normal” supervised learning must infer concepts indirectly from training examples.

[P09-S00245] PDF pages [9]
Context-less example-based learning has the drawback that many different hypotheses can be consistent with the data, especially in the one-shot case.

[P09-S00246] PDF pages [9]
A single image often contains many different visual concepts.

[P09-S00247] PDF pages [9]
Although a capable learner is able to exploit visual cues and heuristics, such as assuming that the concept being demonstrated is the primary object in an image, there is no guarantee.

[P09-S00248] PDF pages [9]
A potential resolution of this discrepancy between zero-shot and few-shot performance is to use CLIP's zero-shot classifier as a prior for the weights of the few-shot classifier.

[P09-S00249] PDF pages [9]
While adding an L2 penalty towards the generated weights is a straightforward implementation of this idea, we found that hyperparameter optimization would often select for such a large value of this regularizer that the resulting few-shot classifier was “just” the zero-shot classifier.

[P09-S00250] PDF pages [9]
Research into better methods of combining the strength of zero-shot transfer with flexibility of few-shot learning is a promising direction for future work.

[P09-S00251] PDF pages [9]
When comparing zero-shot CLIP to few-shot logistic re-gression on the features of other models, zero-shot CLIP roughly matches the performance of the best performing 16-shot classifier in our evaluation suite, which uses the features of a BiT-M ResNet-152x2 trained on ImageNet-21K.

[P09-S00252] PDF pages [9]
We are certain that a BiT-L model trained on JFT-300M would perform even better but these models have not been publicly released.

[P09-S00253] PDF pages [9]
That a BiT-M ResNet-152x2 performs best in a 16-shot setting is somewhat surprising since, as analyzed in Section 3.2, the Noisy Student EfficientNet-L2 outperforms it in a fully supervised setting by almost 5% on average across 27 datasets.

[P09-S00254] PDF pages [9]
In addition to studying the average performance of zero-shot CLIP and few-shot logistic regression, we also examine performance on individual datasets.

[P09-S00255] PDF pages [9]
In Figure 7, we show estimates for the number of labeled examples per class that a logistic regression classifier on the same feature space requires to match the performance of zero-shot CLIP.

[P09-S00256] PDF pages [9]
Since zero-shot CLIP is also a linear classifier, this estimates the effective data efficiency of zero-shot transfer in this setting.

[P09-S00257] PDF pages [9]
In order to avoid training thousands of linear classifiers, we estimate the effective data efficiency based on a loglinear interpolation of the performance of a 1, 2, 4, 8, 16- shot (when possible), and a fully supervised linear classifier trained on each dataset.

[P09-S00258] PDF pages [10]
The data efficiency of zero-shot transfer varies widely.

[P09-S00259] PDF pages [10]
Calculating the number of labeled examples per class a linear classifier on the same CLIP feature space requires to match the performance of the zero-shot classifier contextualizes the effectiveness of zero-shot transfer.

[P09-S00260] PDF pages [10]
Values are estimated based on log-linear interpolation of 1, 2, 4, 8, 16-shot and fully supervised results.

[P09-S00261] PDF pages [10]
Performance varies widely from still underperforming a one-shot classifier on two datasets to matching an estimated 184 labeled examples per class.

[P09-S00262] PDF pages [10]
Two datasets, Flowers102 and EuroSAT underperform one-shot models.

[P09-S00263] PDF pages [10]
Half of the datasets require less than 5 examples per class with a median of 5.4.

[P09-S00264] PDF pages [10]
However, the mean estimated data efficiency is 20.8 examples per class.

[P09-S00265] PDF pages [10]
This is due to the 20% of datasets where supervised classifiers require many labeled examples per class in order to match performance.

[P09-S00266] PDF pages [10]
On ImageNet, zero-shot CLIP matches the performance of a 16-shot linear classifier trained on the same feature space.

[P09-S00267] PDF pages [10]
If we assume that evaluation datasets are large enough that the parameters of linear classifiers trained on them are well estimated, then, because CLIP's zero-shot classifier is also a linear classifier, the performance of the fully supervised classifiers roughly sets an upper bound for what zero-shot transfer can achieve.

[P09-S00268] PDF pages [10]
In Figure 8 we compare CLIP's zero-shot performance with fully supervised linear classifiers across datasets.

[P09-S00269] PDF pages [10]
The dashed, y = x line represents an “optimal” zero-shot classifier that matches the performance of its fully supervised equivalent.

[P09-S00270] PDF pages [10]
For most datasets, the performance of zero-shot classifiers still underperform fully supervised classifiers by 10% to 25%, suggesting that there is still plenty of headroom for improving CLIP's task-learning and zero-shot transfer capabilities.

[P09-S00271] PDF pages [10]
Zero-shot performance is correlated with linear probe performance but still mostly sub-optimal.

[P09-S00272] PDF pages [10]
Comparing zero-shot and linear probe performance across datasets shows a strong correlation with zero-shot performance mostly shifted 10 to 25 points lower.

[P09-S00273] PDF pages [10]
On only 5 datasets does zero-shot performance approach linear probe performance (≤3 point difference).

[P09-S00274] PDF pages [10]
However, zero-shot CLIP only approaches fully supervised performance on 5 datasets: STL10, CI- FAR10, Food101, OxfordPets, and Caltech101.

[P09-S00275] PDF pages [10]
On all 5 datasets, both zero-shot accuracy and fully supervised accuracy are over 90%.

[P09-S00276] PDF pages [10]
This suggests that CLIP may be more effective at zero-shot transfer for tasks where its underlying representations are also high quality.

[P09-S00277] PDF pages [10]
The slope of a linear regression model predicting zero-shot performance as a function of fully supervised performance estimates that for every 1% improvement in fully supervised performance, zero-shot performance improves by 1.28%.

[P09-S00278] PDF pages [10]
However, the 95th-percentile confidence intervals still include values of less than 1 (0.93-1.79).

[P09-S00279] PDF pages [10]
Over the past few years, empirical studies of deep learning systems have documented that performance is predictable as a function of important quantities such as training compute and dataset size (Hestness et al., 2017; Kaplan et al., 2020).

[P09-S00280] PDF pages [10]
The GPT family of models has so far demonstrated consistent improvements in zero-shot performance across a 1000x increase in training compute.

[P09-S00281] PDF pages [10]
In Figure 9, we check whether the zero-shot performance of CLIP follows a similar scaling pattern.

[P09-S00282] PDF pages [10]
We plot the average error rate of the 5 ResNet CLIP models across 39 evaluations on 36 different datasets and find that a similar log-log linear scaling trend holds for CLIP across a 44x increase in model compute.

[P09-S00283] PDF pages [10]
While the overall trend is smooth, we found that performance on individual evaluations can be much noisier.

[P09-S00284] PDF pages [11]
Zero-shot CLIP performance scales smoothly as a function of model compute.

[P09-S00285] PDF pages [11]
Across 39 evals on 36 different datasets, average zero-shot error is well modeled by a log-log linear trend across a 44x range of compute spanning 5 different CLIP models.

[P09-S00286] PDF pages [11]
Lightly shaded lines are performance on individual evals, showing that performance is much more varied despite the smooth overall trend.

[P09-S00287] PDF pages [11]
While we have extensively analyzed the task-learning capabilities of CLIP through zero-shot transfer in the previous section, it is more common to study the representation learning capabilities of a model.

[P09-S00288] PDF pages [11]
There exist many ways to evaluate the quality of representations as well as disagreements over what properties an “ideal” representation should have (Locatello et al., 2020).

[P09-S00289] PDF pages [11]
Fitting a linear classifier on a representation extracted from the model and measuring its performance on various datasets is a common approach.

[P09-S00290] PDF pages [11]
An alternative is measuring the performance of end-to-end fine-tuning of the model.

[P09-S00291] PDF pages [11]
This increases flexibility, and prior work has convincingly demonstrated that fine-tuning outperforms linear classification on most image classification datasets (Kornblith et al., 2019; Zhai et al., 2019).

[P09-S00292] PDF pages [11]
While the high performance of fine-tuning motivates its study for practical reasons, we still opt for linear classifier based evaluation for several reasons.

[P09-S00293] PDF pages [11]
Our work is focused on developing a high-performing task and dataset-agnostic pre-training approach.

[P09-S00294] PDF pages [11]
Fine-tuning, because it adapts representations to each dataset during the fine-tuning phase, can compensate for and potentially mask failures to learn general and robust representations during the pre-training phase.

[P09-S00295] PDF pages [11]
Linear classifiers, because of their limited flexibility, instead highlight these failures and provide clear feedback during development.

[P09-S00296] PDF pages [11]
For CLIP, training supervised linear classifiers has the added benefit of being very similar to the approach used for its zero-shot classifiers which enables extensive comparisons and analysis in Section 3.1.

[P09-S00297] PDF pages [11]
Finally, we aim to compare CLIP to a comprehensive set of existing models across many tasks.

[P09-S00298] PDF pages [11]
Studying 66 different models on 27 different datasets requires tuning 1782 different evaluations.

[P09-S00299] PDF pages [11]
Fine-tuning opens up a much larger design and hyperparameter space, which makes it difficult to fairly evaluate and computationally expensive to compare a diverse set of techniques as discussed in other large scale empirical studies (Lucic et al., 2018; Choi et al., 2019).

[P09-S00300] PDF pages [11]
By comparison, linear classifiers require minimal hyper-parameter tuning and have standardized implementations and evaluation procedures.

[P09-S00301] PDF pages [11]
Please see Appendix A for further details on evaluation.

[P09-S00302] PDF pages [11]
To minimize selection effects that could raise concerns of confirmation or reporting bias, we first study performance on the 12 dataset evaluation suite from Kornblith et al. (2019).

[P09-S00303] PDF pages [11]
While small CLIP models such as a ResNet-50 and ResNet-101 outperform other ResNets trained on ImageNet-1K (BiT-S and the originals), they underperform ResNets trained on ImageNet-21K (BiT- M).

[P09-S00304] PDF pages [11]
These small CLIP models also underperform models in the EfficientNet family with similar compute requirements.

[P09-S00305] PDF pages [11]
However, models trained with CLIP scale very well and the largest model we trained (ResNet-50x64) slightly outperforms the best performing existing model (a Noisy Student EfficientNet-L2) on both overall score and compute efficiency.

[P09-S00306] PDF pages [11]
We also find that CLIP vision transformers are about 3x more compute efficient than CLIP ResNets, which allows us to reach higher overall performance within our compute budget.

[P09-S00307] PDF pages [11]
These results qualitatively replicate the findings of Dosovitskiy et al. (2020) which reported that vision transformers are more compute efficient than convnets when trained on sufficiently large datasets.

[P09-S00308] PDF pages [11]
Our best overall model is a ViT-L/14 that is fine-tuned at a higher resolution of 336 pixels on our dataset for 1 additional epoch.

[P09-S00309] PDF pages [11]
This model outperforms the best existing model across this evaluation suite by an average of 2.6%.

[P09-S00310] PDF pages [11]
As Figure 21 qualitatively shows, CLIP models learn a wider set of tasks than has previously been demonstrated in a single computer vision model trained end-to-end from random initialization.

[P09-S00311] PDF pages [11]
These tasks include geo-localization, optical character recognition, facial emotion recognition, and action recognition.

[P09-S00312] PDF pages [11]
None of these tasks are measured in the evaluation suite of Kornblith et al. (2019).

[P09-S00313] PDF pages [11]
This could be argued to be a form of selection bias in Kornblith et al. (2019)'s study towards tasks that overlap with ImageNet.

[P09-S00314] PDF pages [11]
To address this, we also measure performance on a broader 27 dataset evaluation suite.

[P09-S00315] PDF pages [11]
This evaluation suite, detailed in Appendix A includes datasets representing the aforementioned tasks, German Traffic Signs Recognition Benchmark (Stallkamp et al., 2011), as well as several other datasets adapted from VTAB (Zhai et al., 2019).

[P09-S00316] PDF pages [12]
Linear probe performance of CLIP models in comparison with state-of-the-art computer vision models, including EfficientNet (Tan & Le, 2019; Xie et al., 2020), MoCo (Chen et al., 2020d), Instagram-pretrained ResNeXt models (Mahajan et al., 2018; Touvron et al., 2019), BiT (Kolesnikov et al., 2019), ViT (Dosovitskiy et al., 2020), SimCLRv2 (Chen et al., 2020c), BYOL (Grill et al., 2020), and the original ResNet models (He et al., 2016b).

[P09-S00317] PDF pages [12]
(Left) Scores are averaged over 12 datasets studied by Kornblith et al. (2019).

[P09-S00318] PDF pages [12]
(Right) Scores are averaged over 27 datasets that contain a wider variety of distributions.

[P09-S00319] PDF pages [12]
Dotted lines indicate models fine-tuned or evaluated on images at a higher-resolution than pre-training.

[P09-S00320] PDF pages [12]
See Table 10 for individual scores and Figure 20 for plots for each dataset.

[P09-S00321] PDF pages [12]
On this broader evaluation suite, the benefits of CLIP are more clear.

[P09-S00322] PDF pages [12]
All CLIP models, regardless of scale, outperform all evaluated systems in terms of compute efficiency.

[P09-S00323] PDF pages [12]
The improvement in average score of the best model over previous systems increases from 2.6% to 5%.

[P09-S00324] PDF pages [12]
We also find that self-supervised systems do noticeably better on our broader evaluation suite.

[P09-S00325] PDF pages [12]
For instance, while SimCLRv2 still underperforms BiT-M on average on the 12 datasets of Kornblith et al. (2019), SimCLRv2 outperforms BiT-M on our 27 dataset evaluation suite.

[P09-S00326] PDF pages [12]
These findings suggest continuing to expand task diversity and coverage in order to better understand the “general” performance of systems.

[P09-S00327] PDF pages [12]
We suspect additional evaluation efforts along the lines of VTAB to be valuable.

[P09-S00328] PDF pages [12]
In addition to the aggregate analysis above, we visualize per-dataset differences in the performance of the best CLIP model and the best model in our evaluation suite across all 27 datasets in Figure 11.

[P09-S00329] PDF pages [12]
CLIP outperforms the Noisy Student EfficientNet-L2 on 21 of the 27 datasets.

[P09-S00330] PDF pages [12]
CLIP improves the most on tasks which require OCR (SST2 and HatefulMemes), geo-localization and scene recognition (Country211, SUN397), and activity recognition in videos (Kinetics700 and UCF101).

[P09-S00331] PDF pages [12]
In addition CLIP also does much better on fine-grained car and traffic sign recognition (Stanford Cars and GTSRB).

[P09-S00332] PDF pages [12]
This may reflect a problem with overly narrow supervision in ImageNet.

[P09-S00333] PDF pages [12]
A result such as the 14.7% improvement on GTSRB could be indicative of an issue with ImageNet-1K, which has only a single label for all traffic and street signs.

[P09-S00334] PDF pages [12]
This could encourage a supervised representation to collapse intra-class details and hurt accuracy on a fine-grained downstream task.

[P09-S00335] PDF pages [12]
As mentioned, CLIP still underperforms the EfficientNet on several datasets.

[P09-S00336] PDF pages [12]
Unsurprisingly, the dataset that the EfficientNet does best relative to CLIP on is the one it was trained on: ImageNet.

[P09-S00337] PDF pages [12]
The EffcientNet also slightly outperforms CLIP on low-resolution datasets such as CIFAR10 and CIFAR100.

[P09-S00338] PDF pages [12]
We suspect this is at least partly due to the lack of scale-based data augmentation in CLIP.

[P09-S00339] PDF pages [13]
CLIP's features outperform the features of the best ImageNet model on a wide variety of datasets.

[P09-S00340] PDF pages [13]
Fitting a linear classifier on CLIP's features outperforms using the Noisy Student EfficientNet-L2 on 21 out of 27 datasets.

[P09-S00341] PDF pages [13]
In 2015, it was announced that a deep learning model exceeded human performance on the ImageNet test set (He et al., 2015).

[P09-S00342] PDF pages [13]
However, research in the subsequent years has repeatedly found that these models still make many simple mistakes (Dodge & Karam, 2017; Geirhos et al., 2018; Alcorn et al., 2019), and new benchmarks testing these systems has often found their performance to be much lower than both their ImageNet accuracy and human accuracy (Recht et al., 2019; Barbu et al., 2019).

[P09-S00343] PDF pages [13]
Various ideas have been suggested and studied (Ilyas et al., 2019; Geirhos et al., 2020).

[P09-S00344] PDF pages [13]
A common theme of proposed explanations is that deep learning models are exceedingly adept at finding correlations and patterns which hold across their training dataset and thus improve in-distribution performance.

[P09-S00345] PDF pages [13]
However many of these correlations and patterns are actually spurious and do not hold for other distributions and result in large drops in performance on other datasets.

[P09-S00346] PDF pages [13]
We caution that, to date, most of these studies limit their evaluation to models trained on ImageNet.

[P09-S00347] PDF pages [13]
Recalling the topic of discussion, it may be a mistake to generalize too far from these initial findings.

[P09-S00348] PDF pages [13]
To what degree are these failures attributable to deep learning, ImageNet, or some combination of the two?

[P09-S00349] PDF pages [13]
CLIP models, which are trained via natural language supervision on a very large dataset and are capable of high zero-shot performance, are an opportunity to investigate this question from a different angle.

[P09-S00350] PDF pages [13]
Taori et al. (2020) is a recent comprehensive study moving towards quantifying and understanding these behaviors for ImageNet models.

[P09-S00351] PDF pages [13]
Taori et al. (2020) study how the performance of ImageNet models change when evaluated on natural distribution shifts.

[P09-S00352] PDF pages [13]
They measure performance on a set of 7 distribution shifts: ImageNetV2 (Recht et al., 2019), ImageNet Sketch (Wang et al., 2019), Youtube-BB and ImageNet-Vid (Shankar et al., 2019), ObjectNet (Barbu et al., 2019), ImageNet Adversarial (Hendrycks et al., 2019), and ImageNet Rendition (Hendrycks et al., 2020a).

[P09-S00353] PDF pages [13]
They distinguish these datasets, which all consist of novel images collected from a variety of sources, from synthetic distribution shifts such as ImageNet-C (Hendrycks & Dietterich, 2019), Stylized ImageNet (Geirhos et al., 2018), or adversarial attacks (Goodfellow et al., 2014) which are created by perturbing existing images in various ways.

[P09-S00354] PDF pages [13]
They propose this distinction because in part because they find that while several techniques have been demonstrated to improve performance on synthetic distribution shifts, they often fail to yield consistent improvements on natural distributions.3 Across these collected datasets, the accuracy of ImageNet models drop well below the expectation set by the ImageNet validation set.

[P09-S00355] PDF pages [13]
For the following summary discussion we report average accuracy across all 7 natural distribution shift datasets and average accuracy across the corresponding class subsets of ImageNet unless otherwise specified.

[P09-S00356] PDF pages [13]
Additionally, for Youtube-BB and ImageNet-Vid, which have two different evaluation settings, we use the average of pm-0 and pm-10 accuracy.

[P09-S00357] PDF pages [13]
A ResNet-101 makes 5 times as many mistakes when evaluated on these natural distribution shifts compared to the ImageNet validation set.

[P09-S00358] PDF pages [13]
Encouragingly however, Taori et al. (2020) find that accuracy under distribution shift increases predictably with ImageNet accuracy and is well modeled as a linear function of logit-transformed accuracy.

[P09-S00359] PDF pages [13]
Taori et al. (2020) use this finding to propose that robustness analysis should distinguish between effective and relative robustness.

[P09-S00360] PDF pages [13]
Effective robustness measures improvements in accuracy under distribution shift above what is predicted by the documented relationship between in-distribution and out-of-distribution accuracy.

[P09-S00361] PDF pages [13]
Relative robustness captures any improvement in out-of-distribution accuracy.

[P09-S00362] PDF pages [13]
Taori et al. (2020) argue that robustness techniques should aim to improve both effective robustness and relative robustness.

[P09-S00363] PDF pages [13]
Almost all models studied in Taori et al. (2020) are trained 3We refer readers to Hendrycks et al. (2020a) for additional experiments and discussion on this claim.

[P09-S00364] PDF pages [14]
CLIP's features are more robust to task shift when compared to models pre-trained on ImageNet.

[P09-S00365] PDF pages [14]
For both dataset splits, the transfer scores of linear probes trained on the representations of CLIP models are higher than other models with similar ImageNet performance.

[P09-S00366] PDF pages [14]
This suggests that the representations of models trained on ImageNet are somewhat overfit to their task.

[P09-S00367] PDF pages [14]
Returning to the discussion in the introduction to this section - is training or adapting to the ImageNet dataset distribution the cause of the observed robustness gap?

[P09-S00368] PDF pages [14]
Intuitively, a zero-shot model should not be able to exploit spurious correlations or patterns that hold only on a specific distribution, since it is not trained on that distribution.

[P09-S00369] PDF pages [14]
In Figure 13, we compare the performance of zero-shot CLIP with existing ImageNet models on natural distribution shifts.

[P09-S00370] PDF pages [14]
All zero-shot CLIP models improve effective robustness by a large amount and reduce the size of the gap between ImageNet accuracy and accuracy under distribution shift by up to 75%.

[P09-S00371] PDF pages [14]
While these results show that zero-shot models can be much more robust, they do not necessarily mean that supervised learning on ImageNet causes a robustness gap.

[P09-S00372] PDF pages [14]
Other details of CLIP, such as its large and diverse pre-training dataset or use of natural language supervision could also result 4We caution that a zero-shot model can still exploit spurious correlations that are shared between the pre-training and evaluation distributions.

[P09-S00373] PDF pages [14]
As an initial experiment to potentially begin narrowing this down, we also measure how the performance of CLIP models change after adapting to the ImageNet distribution via a L2 regularized logistic regression classifier fit to CLIP features on the ImageNet training set.

[P09-S00374] PDF pages [14]
We visualize how performance changes from the zero-shot classifier in Figure 14.

[P09-S00375] PDF pages [14]
Although adapting CLIP to the ImageNet distribution increases its ImageNet accuracy by 9.2% to 85.4% overall, and ties the accuracy of the 2018 SOTA from Mahajan et al. (2018), average accuracy under distribution shift slightly decreases.

[P09-S00376] PDF pages [14]
It is surprising to see a 9.2% increase in accuracy, which corresponds to roughly 3 years of improvement in SOTA, fail to translate into any improvement in average performance under distribution shift.

[P09-S00377] PDF pages [14]
We also break down the differences between zero-shot accuracy and linear classifier accuracy per dataset in Figure 14 and find performance still increases significantly on one dataset, ImageNetV2.

[P09-S00378] PDF pages [14]
ImageNetV2 closely followed the creation process of the original ImageNet dataset which suggests that gains in accuracy from supervised adaptation are closely concentrated around the ImageNet distribution.

[P09-S00379] PDF pages [15]
Zero-shot CLIP is much more robust to distribution shift than standard ImageNet models.

[P09-S00380] PDF pages [15]
(Left) An ideal robust model (dashed line) performs equally well on the ImageNet distribution and on other natural image distributions.

[P09-S00381] PDF pages [15]
Zero-shot CLIP models shrink this “robustness gap” by up to 75%.

[P09-S00382] PDF pages [15]
Linear fits on logit transformed values are shown with bootstrap estimated 95% confidence intervals.

[P09-S00383] PDF pages [15]
(Right) Visualizing distribution shift for bananas, a class shared across 5 of the 7 natural distribution shift datasets.

[P09-S00384] PDF pages [15]
The performance of the best zero-shot CLIP model, ViT-L/14@336px, is compared with a model that has the same performance on the ImageNet validation set, ResNet-101.

[P09-S00385] PDF pages [15]
ImageNet-R, 3.8% on ObjectNet, 2.8% on ImageNet Sketch, and 1.9% on ImageNet-A.

[P09-S00386] PDF pages [15]
The change in accuracy on the two other datasets, Youtube-BB and ImageNet Vid, is in-significant.

[P09-S00387] PDF pages [15]
How is it possible to improve accuracy by 9.2% on the ImageNet dataset with little to no increase in accuracy under distribution shift?

[P09-S00388] PDF pages [15]
Is the gain primarily from “exploiting spurious correlations”?

[P09-S00389] PDF pages [15]
Is this behavior unique to some combination of CLIP, the ImageNet datatset, and the distribution shifts studied, or a more general phenomena?

[P09-S00390] PDF pages [15]
Does it hold for end-to-end finetuning as well as linear classifiers?

[P09-S00391] PDF pages [15]
We do not have confident answers to these questions at this time.

[P09-S00392] PDF pages [15]
Prior work has also pre-trained models on distributions other than ImageNet, but it is common to study and release models only after they have been fine-tuned to ImageNet.

[P09-S00393] PDF pages [15]
As a step towards understanding whether pre-trained zero-shot models consistently have higher effective robustness than fine-tuned models, we encourage the authors of Mahajan et al. (2018), Kolesnikov et al. (2019), and Dosovitskiy et al. (2020) to, if possible, study these questions on their models as well.

[P09-S00394] PDF pages [15]
We also investigate another robustness intervention enabled by flexible zero-shot natural-language-based image classifiers.

[P09-S00395] PDF pages [15]
The target classes across the 7 transfer datasets are not always perfectly aligned with those of ImageNet.

[P09-S00396] PDF pages [15]
Two datasets, Youtube-BB and ImageNet-Vid, consist of superclasses of ImageNet.

[P09-S00397] PDF pages [15]
This presents a problem when trying to use the fixed 1000-way classifier of an ImageNet model to make predictions.

[P09-S00398] PDF pages [15]
Taori et al. (2020) handle this by max- pooling predictions across all sub-classes according to the ImageNet class hierarchy.

[P09-S00399] PDF pages [15]
Sometimes this mapping is much less than perfect.

[P09-S00400] PDF pages [15]
For the person class in Youtube-BB, pre-dictions are made by pooling over the ImageNet classes for a baseball player, a bridegroom, and a scuba diver.

[P09-S00401] PDF pages [15]
With CLIP we can instead generate a custom zero-shot classifier for each dataset directly based on its class names.

[P09-S00402] PDF pages [15]
In Figure 14 we see that this improves average effective robustness by 5% but is concentrated in large improvements on only a few datasets.

[P09-S00403] PDF pages [15]
Curiously, accuracy on ObjectNet also increases by 2.3%.

[P09-S00404] PDF pages [15]
Although the dataset was designed to closely overlap with ImageNet classes, using the names provided for each class by ObjectNet's creators still helps a small amount compared to using ImageNet class names and pooling predictions when necessary.

[P09-S00405] PDF pages [15]
While zero-shot CLIP improves effective robustness, Figure 14 shows that the benefit is almost entirely gone in a fully supervised setting.

[P09-S00406] PDF pages [15]
To better understand this difference, we investigate how effective robustness changes on the continuum from zero-shot to fully supervised.

[P09-S00407] PDF pages [15]
We see that while few-shot models also show higher effective robustness than existing models, this benefit fades as in-distribution performance increases with more training data and is mostly, though not entirely, gone for the fully supervised model.

[P09-S00408] PDF pages [15]
Additionally, zero-shot CLIP is notably more robust than a few-shot model with equivalent ImageNet performance.

[P09-S00409] PDF pages [16]
While supervised adaptation to ImageNet increases ImageNet accuracy by 9.2%, it slightly reduces average robustness.

[P09-S00410] PDF pages [16]
(Left) Customizing zero-shot CLIP to each dataset improves robustness compared to using a single static zero-shot ImageNet classifier and pooling predictions across similar classes as in Taori et al. (2020).

[P09-S00411] PDF pages [16]
CLIP models adapted to ImageNet have similar effective robustness as the best prior ImageNet models.

[P09-S00412] PDF pages [16]
(Right) Details of per dataset changes in accuracy for the two robustness interventions.

[P09-S00413] PDF pages [16]
Adapting to ImageNet increases accuracy on ImageNetV2 noticeably but trades off accuracy on several other distributions.

[P09-S00414] PDF pages [16]
Dataset specific zero-shot classifiers can improve accuracy by a large amount but are limited to only a few datasets that include classes which don't perfectly align with ImageNet categories.

[P09-S00415] PDF pages [16]
Across our experiments, high effective robustness seems to result from minimizing the amount of distribution specific training data a model has access to, but this comes at a cost of reducing dataset-specific performance.

[P09-S00416] PDF pages [16]
Taken together, these results suggest that the recent shift towards large-scale task and dataset agnostic pre-training combined with a reorientation towards zero-shot and few-shot benchmarking on broad evaluation suites (as advocated by Yogatama et al. (2019) and Linzen (2020)) promotes the development of more robust systems and provides a more accurate assessment of performance.

[P09-S00417] PDF pages [16]
We are curious to see if the same results hold for zero-shot models in the field of NLP such as the GPT family.

[P09-S00418] PDF pages [16]
While Hendrycks et al. (2020b) has reported that pre-training improves relative robustness on sentiment analysis, Miller et al. (2020)'s study of the robustness of question answering models under natural distribution shift finds, similar to Taori et al. (2020), little evidence of effective robustness improvements to date.

[P09-S00419] PDF pages [16]
How does CLIP compare to human performance and human learning?

[P09-S00420] PDF pages [16]
To get a better understanding of how well humans perform in similar evaluation settings to CLIP, we evaluated humans on one of our tasks.

[P09-S00421] PDF pages [16]
We wanted to get a sense of how strong human zero-shot performance is at these tasks, and how much human performance is improved if they are shown one or two image samples.

[P09-S00422] PDF pages [16]
This can help us to compare task difficulty for humans and CLIP, and identify correlations and differences between them.

[P09-S00423] PDF pages [16]
We had five different humans look at each of 3669 images in the test split of the Oxford IIT Pets dataset (Parkhi et al., 2012) and select which of the 37 cat or dog breeds best matched the image (or 'I don't know' if they were completely uncertain).

[P09-S00424] PDF pages [16]
In the zero-shot case the humans were given no examples of the breeds and asked to label them to the best of their ability without an internet search.

[P09-S00425] PDF pages [16]
In the one-shot experiment the humans were given one sample image of each breed and in the two-shot experiment they were given two sample images of each breed.5 One possible concern was that the human workers were not sufficiently motivated in the zero-shot task.

[P09-S00426] PDF pages [16]
High human accuracy of 94% on the STL-10 dataset (Coates et al., 2011) 5There is not a perfect correspondence between the human few-shot tasks and the model's few-shot performance since the model cannot refer to sample images in the way that the humans can.

[P09-S00427] PDF pages [17]
Few-shot CLIP also increases effective robustness compared to existing ImageNet models but is less robust than zero-shot CLIP.

[P09-S00428] PDF pages [17]
Minimizing the amount of ImageNet training data used for adaption increases effective robustness at the cost of decreasing relative robustness.

[P09-S00429] PDF pages [17]
Interestingly, humans went from a performance average of 54% to 76% with just one training example per class, and the marginal gain from an additional training example is minimal.

[P09-S00430] PDF pages [17]
The gain in accuracy going from zero to one shot is almost entirely on images that humans were uncertain about.

[P09-S00431] PDF pages [17]
This suggests that humans “know what they don't know” and are able to update their priors on the images they are most uncertain in based on a single example.

[P09-S00432] PDF pages [17]
Given this, it seems that while CLIP is a promising training strategy for zero-shot performance (Figure 5) and does well on tests of natural distribution shift (Figure 13), there is a large difference between how humans learn from a few examples and the few-shot methods in this paper.

[P09-S00433] PDF pages [17]
This suggests that there are still algorithmic improvements waiting to be made to decrease the gap between machine and human sample efficiency, as noted by Lake et al. (2016) and others.

[P09-S00434] PDF pages [17]
Because these few-shot evaluations of CLIP don't make effective use of prior knowledge and the humans do, we speculate that finding a method to properly integrate prior knowledge into few-shot learning is an important step in algorithmic improvements to CLIP.

[P09-S00435] PDF pages [17]
Comparison of human performance on Oxford IIT Pets.

[P09-S00436] PDF pages [17]
As in Parkhi et al. (2012), the metric is average per-class classification accuracy.

[P09-S00437] PDF pages [17]
Most of the gain in performance when going from the human zero shot case to the human one shot case is on images that participants were highly uncertain on.

[P09-S00438] PDF pages [17]
If we plot human accuracy vs CLIP's zero shot accuracy (Figure 16), we see that the hardest problems for CLIP are also hard for humans.

[P09-S00439] PDF pages [17]
To the extent that errors are consistent, our hypothesis is that this is due to at least a two factors: noise in the dataset (including mislabeled images) and out of distribution images being hard for both humans and models.

[P09-S00440] PDF pages [17]
A concern with pre-training on a very large internet dataset is unintentional overlap with downstream evals.

[P09-S00441] PDF pages [17]
This is important to investigate since, in a worst-case scenario, a complete copy of an evaluation dataset could leak into the pre-training dataset and invalidate the evaluation as a meaningful test of generalization.

[P09-S00442] PDF pages [17]
One option to prevent this is to identify and remove all duplicates before training a model.

[P09-S00443] PDF pages [17]
While this guarantees reporting true hold-out performance, it requires knowing all possible data which a model might be evaluated on ahead of time.

[P09-S00444] PDF pages [17]
This has the downside of limiting the scope of benchmarking and analysis.

[P09-S00445] PDF pages [17]
Adding a new evaluation would require an expensive re-train or risk reporting an un-quantified benefit due to overlap.

[P09-S00446] PDF pages [17]
Instead, we document how much overlap occurs and how performance changes due to these overlaps.

[P09-S00447] PDF pages [17]
To do this, we use the following procedure: 1) For each evaluation dataset, we run a duplicate detector (see Appendix C) on its examples.

[P09-S00448] PDF pages [17]
We then manually inspect the found nearest neighbors and set a per dataset threshold to keep high precision while maximizing recall.

[P09-S00449] PDF pages [18]
The hardest problems for CLIP also tend to be the hard- est problems for humans.

[P09-S00450] PDF pages [18]
Here we rank image categories by difficulty for CLIP as measured as probability of the correct label.

[P09-S00451] PDF pages [18]
We denote the unaltered full dataset All for reference.

[P09-S00452] PDF pages [18]
From this we first record the degree of data contamination as the ratio of the number of examples in Overlap to the size of All.

[P09-S00453] PDF pages [18]
This is the difference in accuracy due to contamination.

[P09-S00454] PDF pages [18]
When positive it is our estimate of how much the overall reported accuracy on the dataset was in-flated by over-fitting to overlapping data.

[P09-S00455] PDF pages [18]
We also calculate 99.5% Clopper-Pearson confidence intervals on Dirty as another check.

[P09-S00456] PDF pages [18]
A summary of this analysis is presented in Figure 17.

[P09-S00457] PDF pages [18]
Out of 35 datasets studied, 9 datasets have no detected overlap at all.

[P09-S00458] PDF pages [18]
Most of these datasets are synthetic or specialized making them unlikely to be posted as normal images on the internet (for instance MNIST, CLEVR, and GTSRB) or are guaranteed to have no overlap due to containing novel data from after the date our dataset was created (ObjectNet and Hateful Memes).

[P09-S00459] PDF pages [18]
This demonstrates our detector has a low-false positive rate which is important as false positives would under-estimate the effect of contamination in our analysis.

[P09-S00460] PDF pages [18]
There is a median overlap of 2.2% and an average overlap of 3.2%.

[P09-S00461] PDF pages [18]
Due to this small amount of overlap, overall accuracy is rarely shifted by more than 0.1% with only 7 datasets above this threshold.

[P09-S00462] PDF pages [18]
Of these, only 2 are statistically significant after Bonferroni correction.

[P09-S00463] PDF pages [18]
The max detected improvement is only 0.6% on Birdsnap which has the second largest overlap at 12.1%.

[P09-S00464] PDF pages [18]
The largest overlap is for Country211 at 21.5%.

[P09-S00465] PDF pages [18]
This is due to it being constructed out of YFCC100M, which our pre-training dataset contains a filtered subset of.

[P09-S00466] PDF pages [18]
Despite this large overlap there is only a 0.2% increase in accuracy on Country211.

[P09-S00467] PDF pages [18]
This may be because the training text accompanying an example is often not related to the specific task a downstream eval measures.

[P09-S00468] PDF pages [18]
Country211 measures geo-localization ability, but inspecting the training text for these duplicates showed they often do not mention the location of the image.

[P09-S00469] PDF pages [18]
We are aware of two potential concerns with our analysis.

[P09-S00470] PDF pages [18]
First our detector is not perfect.

[P09-S00471] PDF pages [18]
While it achieves near 100% accuracy on its proxy training task and manual in-spection + threshold tuning results in very high precision with good recall among the found nearest-neighbors, we can not tractably check its recall across 400 million examples.

[P09-S00472] PDF pages [18]
Another potential confounder of our analysis is that the underlying data distribution may shift between the Overlap and Clean subsets.

[P09-S00473] PDF pages [18]
For example, on Kinetics-700 many “overlaps” are in fact all black transition frames.

[P09-S00474] PDF pages [18]
This ex- plains why Kinetics-700 has an apparent 20% accuracy drop on Overlap.

[P09-S00475] PDF pages [18]
We suspect more subtle distribution shifts likely exist.

[P09-S00476] PDF pages [18]
One possibility we noticed on CIFAR-100 is that, due to the very low resolution of its images, many duplicates were false positives of small objects such as birds or planes.

[P09-S00477] PDF pages [18]
Changes in accuracy could instead be due to changes in the class distribution or difficulty of the duplicates.

[P09-S00478] PDF pages [18]
Unfortunately, these distribution and difficulty shifts could also mask the effects of over-fitting.

[P09-S00479] PDF pages [18]
However, these results closely follow the findings of similar duplicate analysis in previous work on large scale pre-training.

[P09-S00480] PDF pages [18]
Mahajan et al. (2018) and Kolesnikov et al. (2019) detected similar overlap rates and found minimal changes in overall performance.

[P09-S00481] PDF pages [18]
Importantly, Kolesnikov et al. (2019) also compared the alternative de-duplication strategy discussed in the introduction to this section with the approach we settled on and observed little difference between the two approaches.

[P09-S00482] PDF pages [18]
There are still many limitations to CLIP.

[P09-S00483] PDF pages [18]
While several of these are discussed as part of analysis in various sections, we summarize and collect them here.

[P09-S00484] PDF pages [19]
Few statistically significant improvements in accuracy due to detected data overlap.

[P09-S00485] PDF pages [19]
(Left) While several datasets have up to ±20% apparent differences in zero-shot accuracy on detected overlapping vs clean examples only 5 datasets out of 35 total have 99.5% Clopper-Pearson confidence intervals that exclude a 0% accuracy difference.

[P09-S00486] PDF pages [19]
(Right) Since the percentage of detected overlapping examples is almost always in the single digits, the overall test accuracy gain due to overlap is much smaller with the largest estimated increase being only 0.6% on Birdsnap.

[P09-S00487] PDF pages [19]
Similarly, for only 6 datasets are the accuracy improvements statistically significant when calculated using a one-sided binomial test.

[P09-S00488] PDF pages [19]
On most of these datasets, the performance of this baseline is now well below the overall state of the art.

[P09-S00489] PDF pages [19]
Significant work is still needed to improve the task learning and transfer capabilities of CLIP.

[P09-S00490] PDF pages [19]
While scaling has so far steadily improved performance and suggests a route for continued improvement, we estimate around a 1000x increase in compute is required for zero-shot CLIP to reach overall state-of-the-art performance.

[P09-S00491] PDF pages [19]
This is infeasible to train with current hardware.

[P09-S00492] PDF pages [19]
Further research into improving upon the computational and data efficiency of CLIP will be necessary.

[P09-S00493] PDF pages [19]
Analysis in Section 3.1 found that CLIP's zero-shot performance is still quite weak on several kinds of tasks.

[P09-S00494] PDF pages [19]
When compared to task-specific models, the performance of CLIP is poor on several types of fine-grained classification such as differentiating models of cars, species of flowers, and variants of aircraft.

[P09-S00495] PDF pages [19]
CLIP also struggles with more abstract and systematic tasks such as counting the number of objects in an image.

[P09-S00496] PDF pages [19]
Finally for novel tasks which are unlikely to be included in CLIP's pre-training dataset, such as classifying the distance to the nearest car in a photo, CLIP's performance can be near random.

[P09-S00497] PDF pages [19]
We are confident that there are still many, many, tasks where CLIP's zero-shot performance is near chance level.

[P09-S00498] PDF pages [19]
While zero-shot CLIP generalizes well to many natural image distributions as investigated in Section 3.3, we've observed that zero-shot CLIP still generalizes poorly to data that is truly out-of-distribution for it.

[P09-S00499] PDF pages [19]
An illustrative example occurs for the task of OCR as reported in Appendix E.

[P09-S00500] PDF pages [19]
CLIP learns a high quality semantic OCR representation that performs well on digitally rendered text, which is common in its pre-training dataset, as evidenced by performance on Rendered SST2.

[P09-S00501] PDF pages [19]
However, CLIP only achieves 88% accuracy on the handwritten digits of MNIST.

[P09-S00502] PDF pages [19]
An embarrassingly simple baseline of logistic regression on raw pixels outperforms zero-shot CLIP.

[P09-S00503] PDF pages [19]
Both semantic and near-duplicate nearest-neighbor retrieval verify that there are almost no images that resemble MNIST digits in our pre-training dataset.

[P09-S00504] PDF pages [19]
This suggests CLIP does little to address the underlying problem of brittle generalization of deep learning models.

[P09-S00505] PDF pages [19]
Instead CLIP tries to circumvent the problem and hopes that by training on such a large and varied dataset that all data will be effectively in-distribution.

[P09-S00506] PDF pages [19]
This is a naive assumption that, as MNIST demonstrates, is easy to violate.

[P09-S00507] PDF pages [19]
Although CLIP can flexibly generate zero-shot classifiers for a wide variety of tasks and datasets, CLIP is still limited to choosing from only those concepts in a given zero-shot classifier.

[P09-S00508] PDF pages [19]
This is a significant restriction compared to a truly flexible approach like image captioning which could generate novel outputs.

[P09-S00509] PDF pages [19]
Unfortunately, as described in Section 2.3 we found the computational efficiency of the image caption baseline we tried to be much lower than CLIP.

[P09-S00510] PDF pages [19]
A simple idea worth trying is joint training of a contrastive and generative objective with the hope of combining the efficiency of CLIP with the flexibility of a caption model.

[P09-S00511] PDF pages [19]
As another alternative, search could be performed at inference time over many natural language explanations of a given image, similar to approach proposed in Learning with Latent Language Andreas et al. (2017).

[P09-S00512] PDF pages [20]
Learning Transferable Visual Models From Natural Language Supervision 20 CLIP also does not address the poor data efficiency of deep learning.

[P09-S00513] PDF pages [20]
Instead CLIP compensates by using a source of supervision that can be scaled to hundreds of millions of training examples.

[P09-S00514] PDF pages [20]
If every image seen during training of a CLIP model was presented at a rate of one per second, it would take 405 years to iterate through the 12.8 billion images seen over 32 training epochs.

[P09-S00515] PDF pages [20]
Combining CLIP with self-supervision (Henaff, 2020; Chen et al., 2020c) and self-training (Lee; Xie et al., 2020) methods is a promising direction given their demonstrated ability to improve data efficiency over standard supervised learning.

[P09-S00516] PDF pages [20]
Our methodology has several significant limitations.

[P09-S00517] PDF pages [20]
Despite our focus on zero-shot transfer, we repeatedly queried performance on full validation sets to guide the development of CLIP.

[P09-S00518] PDF pages [20]
These validation sets often have thousands of examples, which is unrealistic for true zero-shot scenarios.

[P09-S00519] PDF pages [20]
Similar concerns have been raised in the field of semi-supervised learning (Oliver et al., 2018).

[P09-S00520] PDF pages [20]
Another potential issue is our selection of evaluation datasets.

[P09-S00521] PDF pages [20]
While we have reported results on Kornblith et al. (2019)'s 12 dataset evaluation suite as a standardized collection, our main results use a somewhat haphazardly assembled collection of 27 datasets that is undeniably co-adapted with the development and capabilities of CLIP.

[P09-S00522] PDF pages [20]
Creating a new benchmark of tasks designed explicitly to evaluate broad zero-shot transfer capabilities, rather than re-using existing supervised datasets, would help address these issues.

[P09-S00523] PDF pages [20]
CLIP is trained on text paired with images on the internet.

[P09-S00524] PDF pages [20]
These image-text pairs are unfiltered and uncurated and result in CLIP models learning many social biases.

[P09-S00525] PDF pages [20]
This has been previously demonstrated for image caption models (Bhargava & Forsyth, 2019).

[P09-S00526] PDF pages [20]
We refer readers to Section 7 for detailed analysis and quantification of these behaviors for CLIP as well as discussion of potential mitigation strategies.

[P09-S00527] PDF pages [20]
While we have emphasized throughout this work that specifying image classifiers through natural language is a flexible and general interface, it has its own limitations.

[P09-S00528] PDF pages [20]
Many complex tasks and visual concepts can be difficult to specify just through text.

[P09-S00529] PDF pages [20]
Actual training examples are undeniably useful but CLIP does not optimize for few-shot performance directly.

[P09-S00530] PDF pages [20]
In our work, we fall back to fitting linear classifiers on top of CLIP's features.

[P09-S00531] PDF pages [20]
This results in a counter-intuitive drop in performance when transitioning from a zero-shot to a few-shot setting.

[P09-S00532] PDF pages [20]
As discussed in Section 4, this is notably different from human performance which shows a large increase from a zero to a one shot setting.

[P09-S00533] PDF pages [20]
Future work is needed to develop methods that combine CLIP's strong zero-shot performance with efficient few-shot learning.

[P09-S00534] PDF pages [20]
CLIP has a wide range of capabilities due to its ability to carry out arbitrary image classification tasks.

[P09-S00535] PDF pages [20]
One can give it images of cats and dogs and ask it to classify cats, or give it images taken in a department store and ask it to classify shoplifters-a task with significant social implications and for which AI may be unfit.

[P09-S00536] PDF pages [20]
Like any image classification system, CLIP's performance and fitness for purpose need to be evaluated, and its broader impacts analyzed in context.

[P09-S00537] PDF pages [20]
CLIP also introduces a capability that will magnify and alter such issues: CLIP makes it possible to easily create your own classes for categorization (to 'roll your own classifier') without a need for re-training.

[P09-S00538] PDF pages [20]
This capability introduces challenges similar to those found in characterizing other, large-scale generative models like GPT-3 (Brown et al., 2020); models that exhibit non-trivial zero-shot (or few-shot) generalization can have a vast range of capabilities, many of which are made clear only after testing for them.

[P09-S00539] PDF pages [20]
Our studies of CLIP in a zero-shot setting show that the model displays significant promise for widely-applicable tasks like image retrieval or search.

[P09-S00540] PDF pages [20]
For example, it can find relevant images in a database given text, or relevant text given an image.

[P09-S00541] PDF pages [20]
Further, the relative ease of steering CLIP toward bespoke applications with little or no additional data or training could unlock a variety of novel applications that are hard for us to envision today, as has occurred with large language models over the past few years.

[P09-S00542] PDF pages [20]
In addition to the more than 30 datasets studied in earlier sections of this paper, we evaluate CLIP's performance on the FairFace benchmark and undertake exploratory bias probes.

[P09-S00543] PDF pages [20]
We then characterize the model's performance in a downstream task, surveillance, and discuss its usefulness as compared with other available systems.

[P09-S00544] PDF pages [20]
Many of CLIP's capabilities are omni-use in nature (e.g. OCR can be used to make scanned documents searchable, to power screen reading technologies, or to read license plates).

[P09-S00545] PDF pages [20]
Several of the capabilities measured, from action recognition, object classification, and geo-localization, to facial emotion recognition, can be used in surveillance.

[P09-S00546] PDF pages [20]
Given its social implications, we address this domain of use specifically in the Surveillance section.
