[P09-S00547] PDF pages [20]
We have also sought to characterize the social biases inherent to the model.

[P09-S00548] PDF pages [20]
Our bias tests represent our initial efforts to probe aspects of how the model responds in different scenarios, and are by nature limited in scope.

[P09-S00549] PDF pages [20]
CLIP and models like it will need to be analyzed in relation to their specific deployments to understand how bias manifests and identify potential interventions.

[P09-S00550] PDF pages [20]
Further community exploration will be required to develop broader, more contextual, and more robust testing schemes so that AI developers can better characterize biases in general purpose computer vision models.

[P09-S00551] PDF pages [21]
Algorithmic decisions, training data, and choices about how classes are defined and taxonomized (which we refer to in-formally as “class design”) can all contribute to and amplify social biases and inequalities resulting from the use of AI systems (Noble, 2018; Bechmann & Bowker, 2019; Bowker & Star, 2000).

[P09-S00552] PDF pages [21]
Class design is particularly relevant to models like CLIP, since any developer can define a class and the model will provide some result.

[P09-S00553] PDF pages [21]
In this section, we provide preliminary analysis of some of the biases in CLIP, using bias probes inspired by those outlined in Buolamwini & Gebru (2018) and K ̈arkk ̈ainen & Joo (2019).

[P09-S00554] PDF pages [21]
We also conduct exploratory bias research intended to find specific examples of biases in the model, similar to that conducted by Solaiman et al. (2019).

[P09-S00555] PDF pages [21]
We start by analyzing the performance of Zero-Shot CLIP on the face image dataset FairFace (K ̈arkk ̈ainen & Joo, 2019)6 6FairFace is a face image dataset designed to balance age, gender, and race, in order to reduce asymmetries common in previous face datasets.

[P09-S00556] PDF pages [21]
It categorizes gender into 2 groups: female and male and race into 7 groups: White, Black, Indian, East Asian, Southeast Asian, Middle Eastern, and Latino.

[P09-S00557] PDF pages [21]
There are inherent problems with race and gender classifications, as e.g. Bowker & Star (2000) as an initial bias probe, then probe the model further to surface additional biases and sources of biases, including class design.

[P09-S00558] PDF pages [21]
We evaluated two versions of CLIP on the FairFace dataset: a zero-shot CLIP model (“ZS CLIP”), and a logistic regression classifier fitted to FairFace's dataset on top of CLIP's features (“LR CLIP”).

[P09-S00559] PDF pages [21]
We find that LR CLIP gets higher accuracy on the FairFace dataset than both the ResNext-101 32x48d Instagram model (“Linear Probe Instagram”) (Mahajan et al., 2018) and FairFace's own model on most of the classification tests we ran7.

[P09-S00560] PDF pages [21]
ZS CLIP's performance varies by category and is worse than that of FairFace's model for a few categories, and better for others.

[P09-S00561] PDF pages [21]
While FairFace's dataset reduces the proportion of White faces, it still lacks representation of entire large demographic groups, effectively erasing such categories.

[P09-S00562] PDF pages [21]
We use the 2 gender categories and 7 race categories defined in the FairFace dataset in a number of our experiments not in order to reinforce or endorse the use of such reductive categories, but in order to enable us to make comparisons to prior work.

[P09-S00563] PDF pages [22]
Learning Transferable Visual Models From Natural Language Supervision 22 Middle Southeast East Category Black White Indian Latino Eastern Asian Asian Crime-related Categories 16.4 24.9 24.4 10.8 19.7 4.4 1.3 Non-human Categories 14.4 5.5 7.6 3.7 2.0 1.9 0.0 Table 6.

[P09-S00564] PDF pages [22]
Percent of images classified into crime-related and non-human categories by FairFace Race category.

[P09-S00565] PDF pages [22]
The label set included 7 FairFace race categories each for men and women (for a total of 14), as well as 3 crime-related categories and 4 non-human categories.

[P09-S00566] PDF pages [22]
Percent of images classified into crime-related and non-human categories by FairFace Age category, showing comparison between results obtained using a default label set and a label set to which the label 'child' has been added.

[P09-S00567] PDF pages [22]
The default label set included 7 FairFace race categories each for men and women (for a total of 14), 3 crime-related categories and 4 non-human categories.

[P09-S00568] PDF pages [22]
Additionally, we test the performance of the LR CLIP and ZS CLIP models across intersectional race and gender categories as they are defined in the FairFace dataset.

[P09-S00569] PDF pages [22]
We find that model performance on gender classification is above 95% for all race categories.

[P09-S00570] PDF pages [22]
While LR CLIP achieves higher accuracy than the Linear Probe Instagram model on the FairFace benchmark dataset for gender, race and age classification of images by intersectional categories, accuracy on benchmarks offers only one approximation of algorithmic fairness, as Raji et al. (2020) have shown, and often fails as a meaningful measure of fairness in real world contexts.

[P09-S00571] PDF pages [22]
Even if a model has both higher accuracy and lower disparities in performance on different sub-groups, this does not mean it will have lower disparities in impact (Scheuerman et al., 2019).

[P09-S00572] PDF pages [22]
For example, higher performance on underrepresented groups might be used by a company to justify their use of facial recognition, and to then deploy it ways that affect demographic groups disproportionately.

[P09-S00573] PDF pages [22]
Our use of facial classification benchmarks to probe for biases is not intended to imply that facial classification is an unproblematic task, nor to endorse the use of race, age, or gender classification in deployed contexts.

[P09-S00574] PDF pages [22]
We also probed the model using classification terms with high potential to cause representational harm, focusing on denigration harms in particular (Crawford, 2017).

[P09-S00575] PDF pages [22]
We carried out an experiment in which the ZS CLIP model was required to classify 10,000 images from the FairFace dataset.

[P09-S00576] PDF pages [22]
In addition to the FairFace classes, we added in the following classes: 'animal', 'gorilla', 'chimpanzee', 'orangutan', 'thief', 'criminal' and 'suspicious person'.

[P09-S00577] PDF pages [22]
The goal of this experiment was to check if harms of denigration disproportionately impact certain demographic subgroups.

[P09-S00578] PDF pages [22]
We found that 4.9% (confidence intervals between 4.6% and 5.4%) of the images were misclassified into one of the non-human classes we used in our probes ('animal', 'chimpanzee', 'gorilla', 'orangutan').

[P09-S00579] PDF pages [22]
Out of these, 'Black' images had the highest misclassification rate (approximately 14%; confidence intervals between [12.6% and 16.4%]) while all other races had misclassification rates under 8%.

[P09-S00580] PDF pages [22]
People aged 0-20 years had the highest proportion being classified into this category at 14% .

[P09-S00581] PDF pages [22]
We also found that 16.5% of male images were misclassified into classes related to crime ('thief', 'suspicious person' and 'criminal') as compared to 9.8% of female images.

[P09-S00582] PDF pages [22]
Inter- estingly, we found that people aged 0-20 years old were more likely to fall under these crime-related classes (approximately 18%) compared to images of people in different age ranges (approximately 12% for people aged 20-60 and 0% for people over 70).

[P09-S00583] PDF pages [22]
We found significant disparities in classifications across races for crime related terms, which is captured in Table 6.

[P09-S00584] PDF pages [22]
Given that we observed that people under 20 were the most likely to be classified in both the crime-related and non-human animal categories, we carried out classification for the images with the same classes but with an additional category 'child' added to the categories.

[P09-S00585] PDF pages [22]
Our goal here was to see if this category would significantly change the behaviour of the model and shift how the denigration harms are distributed by age.

[P09-S00586] PDF pages [22]
We found that this drastically reduced the number of images of people under 20 classified in either crime-related categories or non-human animal categories (Table 7).

[P09-S00587] PDF pages [22, 23]
This points to how class design has the potential to be a key factor determining both the model performance and the unwanted biases or behaviour the model may exhibit while also asks overarching questions about the use of face Learning Transferable Visual Models From Natural Language Supervision 23 images to automatically classify people along such lines (y Arcas et al., 2017).

[P09-S00588] PDF pages [23]
The results of these probes can change based on the class categories one chooses to include as well as the specific language one uses to describe each class.

[P09-S00589] PDF pages [23]
Poor class design can lead to poor real world performance; this concern is particularly relevant to a model like CLIP, given how easily developers can design their own classes.

[P09-S00590] PDF pages [23]
We also carried out experiments similar to those outlined by Schwemmer et al. (2020) to test how CLIP treated images of men and women differently using images of Members of Congress.

[P09-S00591] PDF pages [23]
As part of these experiments, we studied how certain additional design decisions such as deciding thresholds for labels can impact the labels output by CLIP and how biases manifest.

[P09-S00592] PDF pages [23]
We carried out three experiments - we tested for accuracy on gender classification and we tested for how labels were differentially distributed across two different label sets.

[P09-S00593] PDF pages [23]
For our first label set, we used a label set of 300 occupations and for our second label set we used a combined set of labels that Google Cloud Vision, Amazon Rekognition and Microsoft Azure Computer Vision returned for all the images.

[P09-S00594] PDF pages [23]
We first simply looked into gender prediction performance of the model on the images of Members of Congress, in order to check to see if the model correctly recognized men as men and women as women given the image of a person who appeared to be in an official setting/position of power.

[P09-S00595] PDF pages [23]
We found that the model got 100% accuracy on the images.

[P09-S00596] PDF pages [23]
This is slightly better performance than the model's performance on the FairFace dataset.

[P09-S00597] PDF pages [23]
We hypothesize that one of the reasons for this is that all the images in the Members of Congress dataset were high-quality and clear, with the people clearly centered, unlike those in the FairFace dataset.

[P09-S00598] PDF pages [23]
In order to study how the biases in returned labels depend on the thresholds set for label probability, we did an experiment in which we set threshold values at 0.5% and 4.0%.

[P09-S00599] PDF pages [23]
We found that the lower threshold led to lower quality of labels.

[P09-S00600] PDF pages [23]
However, even the differing distributions of labels under this threshold can hold signals for bias.

[P09-S00601] PDF pages [23]
For example, we find that under the 0.5% threshold labels such as 'nanny' and 'housekeeper' start appearing for women whereas labels such as 'prisoner' and 'mobster' start appearing for men.

[P09-S00602] PDF pages [23]
This points to gendered associations similar to those that have previously been found for occupations (Schwemmer et al., 2020) (Nosek et al., 2002) (Bolukbasi et al., 2016).

[P09-S00603] PDF pages [23]
At the higher 4% threshold, the labels with the highest probability across both genders include “lawmaker”, “legislator” and “congressman”.

[P09-S00604] PDF pages [23]
However, the presence of these biases amongst lower probability labels nonetheless point to larger questions about what 'sufficiently' safe behaviour may look like for deploying such systems.

[P09-S00605] PDF pages [23]
When given the combined set of labels that Google Cloud Vision (GCV), Amazon Rekognition and Microsoft returned for all the images, similar to the biases Schwemmer et al. (2020) found in GCV systems, we found our system also disproportionately attached labels to do with hair and appearance in general to women more than men.

[P09-S00606] PDF pages [23]
For example, labels such as 'brown hair', 'blonde' and 'blond' appeared significantly more often for women.

[P09-S00607] PDF pages [23]
Additionally, CLIP attached some labels that described high status occupations disproportionately more often to men such as 'executive' and 'doctor'.

[P09-S00608] PDF pages [23]
Out of the only four occupations that it attached more often to women, three were 'newscaster', 'television presenter' and 'newsreader' and the fourth was 'Judge'.

[P09-S00609] PDF pages [23]
This is again similar to the biases found in GCV and points to historical gendered differences (Schwemmer et al., 2020).

[P09-S00610] PDF pages [23]
Interestingly, when we lowered the threshold to 0.5% for this set of labels, we found that the labels disproportionately describing men also shifted to appearance oriented words such as 'suit', 'tie' and 'necktie' (Figure 18).

[P09-S00611] PDF pages [23]
Many occupation oriented words such as 'military person' and 'executive' - which were not used to describe images of women at the higher 4% threshold - were used for both men and women at the lower 0.5% threshold, which could have caused the change in labels for men.

[P09-S00612] PDF pages [23]
Descriptive words used to describe women were still uncommon amongst men.

[P09-S00613] PDF pages [23]
Design decisions at every stage of building a model impact how biases manifest and this is especially true for CLIP given the flexibility it offers.

[P09-S00614] PDF pages [23]
In addition to choices about training data and model architecture, decisions about things like class designs and thresholding values can alter the labels a model outputs and as a result heighten or lower certain kinds of harm, such as those described by Crawford (2017).

[P09-S00615] PDF pages [23]
People designing and developing models and AI systems have considerable power.

[P09-S00616] PDF pages [23]
Decisions about things like class design are a key determiner not only of model performance, but also of how and in what contexts model biases manifest.

[P09-S00617] PDF pages [23]
They illustrate potential issues stemming from class design and other sources of bias, and are intended to spark inquiry.

[P09-S00618] PDF pages [23]
We next sought to characterize model performance in re-lation to a downstream task for which there is significant societal sensitivity: surveillance.

[P09-S00619] PDF pages [24]
CLIP performance on Member of Congress images when given the combined returned label set for the images from Google Cloud Vision, Amazon Rekognition and Microsoft Azure Computer Vision.

[P09-S00620] PDF pages [24]
The 20 most gendered labels for men and women were identified with χ2 tests with the threshold at 0.5%.

[P09-S00621] PDF pages [24]
Labels are sorted by absolute frequencies.

[P09-S00622] PDF pages [24]
Bars denote the percentage of images for a certain label by gender.

[P09-S00623] PDF pages [24]
Our inclusion of surveillance is not intended to indicate enthusiasm for this domain - rather, we think surveillance is an important domain to try to make predictions about given its societal implications (Zuboff, 2015; Browne, 2015).

[P09-S00624] PDF pages [24]
We measure the model's performance on classification of images from CCTV cameras and zero-shot celebrity identification.

[P09-S00625] PDF pages [24]
We first tested model performance on low-resolution images captured from surveillance cameras (e.g. CCTV cameras).

[P09-S00626] PDF pages [24]
We used the VIRAT dataset (Oh et al., 2011) and data captured by Varadarajan & Odobez (2009), which both consist of real world outdoor scenes with non-actors.

[P09-S00627] PDF pages [24]
Given CLIP's flexible class construction, we tested 515 surveillance images captured from 12 different video sequences on self-constructed general classes for coarse and fine grained classification.

[P09-S00628] PDF pages [24]
Coarse classification required the model to correctly identify the main subject of the image (i.e. determine if the image was a picture of an empty parking lot, school campus, etc.).

[P09-S00629] PDF pages [24]
For fine-grained classification, the model had to choose between two options constructed to determine if the model could identify the presence/absence of smaller features in the image such as a person standing in the corner.

[P09-S00630] PDF pages [24]
For coarse classification, we constructed the classes by handcaptioning the images ourselves to describe the contents of the image and there were always at least 6 options for the model to choose from.

[P09-S00631] PDF pages [24]
Additionally, we carried out a 'stress test' where the class set included at least one more caption for something that was 'close' to the image (for example, 'parking lot with white car' vs. 'parking lot with red car').

[P09-S00632] PDF pages [24]
We found that the model had a top-1 accuracy of 91.8% on the CCTV images for the initial evaluation.

[P09-S00633] PDF pages [24]
The accuracy dropped significantly to 51.1% for the second evaluation, with the model incorrectly choosing the 'close' answer 40.7% of the time.

[P09-S00634] PDF pages [24]
For fine-grained detection, the zero-shot model performed poorly, with results near random.

[P09-S00635] PDF pages [24]
Note that this experiment was targeted only towards detecting the presence or absence of small objects in image sequences.

[P09-S00636] PDF pages [24]
We also tested CLIP's zero-shot performance for 'in the wild' identity detection using the CelebA dataset8.

[P09-S00637] PDF pages [24]
We did this to evaluate the model's performance for identity detection using just the publicly available data it was pre-trained on.

[P09-S00638] PDF pages [24]
While we tested this on a dataset of celebrities who have a larger number of images on the internet, we hypothesize that the number of images in the pre-training data needed for the model to associate faces with names will keep decreasing as models get more powerful (see Table 8), which has significant societal implications (Garvie, 2019).

[P09-S00639] PDF pages [24]
This 8Note: The CelebA dataset is more representative of faces with lighter skin tones.

[P09-S00640] PDF pages [24]
Due to the nature of the dataset, we were not able to control for race, gender, age, etc.

[P09-S00641] PDF pages [25]
CelebA Zero-Shot Top-1 Identity Recognition Accuracy mirrors recent developments in natural language processing, in which recent large language models trained on Internet data often exhibit a surprising ability to provide information related to relatively minor public figures (Brown et al., 2020).

[P09-S00642] PDF pages [25]
We found that the model had 59.2% top-1 accuracy out of 100 possible classes for 'in the wild' 8k celebrity images.

[P09-S00643] PDF pages [25]
However, this performance dropped to 43.3% when we increased our class sizes to 1k celebrity names.

[P09-S00644] PDF pages [25]
This performance is not competitive when compared to production level models such as Google's Celebrity Recognition (Google).

[P09-S00645] PDF pages [25]
However, what makes these results noteworthy is that this analysis was done using only zero-shot identification capabilities based on names inferred from pre-training data - we didn't use any additional task-specific dataset, and so the (relatively) strong results further indicate that before deploying multimodal models, people will need to carefully study them for behaviors in a given context and domain.

[P09-S00646] PDF pages [25]
CLIP offers significant benefit for tasks that have relatively little data given its zero-shot capabilities.

[P09-S00647] PDF pages [25]
However, large datasets and high performing supervised models exist for many in-demand surveillance tasks such as facial recognition.

[P09-S00648] PDF pages [25]
As a result, CLIP's comparative appeal for such uses is low.

[P09-S00649] PDF pages [25]
Additionally, CLIP is not designed for common surveillance-relevant tasks like object detection and semantic segmentation.

[P09-S00650] PDF pages [25]
This means it has limited use for certain surveillance tasks when models that are designed with these uses in mind such as Detectron2 (Wu et al., 2019) are widely available.

[P09-S00651] PDF pages [25]
However, CLIP does unlock a certain aspect of usability given how it removes the need for training data.

[P09-S00652] PDF pages [25]
Thus, CLIP and similar models could enable bespoke, niche surveillance use cases for which no well-tailored models or datasets exist, and could lower the skill requirements to build such applications.

[P09-S00653] PDF pages [25]
As our experiments show, ZS CLIP displays non-trivial, but not exceptional, performance on a few surveillance relevant tasks today.

[P09-S00654] PDF pages [25]
This preliminary analysis is intended to illustrate some of the challenges that general purpose computer vision models pose and to give a glimpse into their biases and impacts.

[P09-S00655] PDF pages [25]
We hope that this work motivates future research on the characterization of the capabilities, shortcomings, and biases of such models, and we are excited to engage with the research community on such questions.

[P09-S00656] PDF pages [25]
We believe one good step forward is community exploration to further characterize the capabilities of models like CLIP and - crucially - identify application areas where they have promising performance and areas where they may have reduced performance9.

[P09-S00657] PDF pages [25]
This process of characterization can help researchers increase the likelihood models are used beneficially by: • Identifying potentially beneficial downstream uses of models early in the research process, enabling other researchers to think about applications.

[P09-S00658] PDF pages [25]
We plan to contribute to this work, and hope this analysis provides some motivating examples for subsequent research.

[P09-S00659] PDF pages [25]
Any model that leverages written, spoken, signed or any other form of human language as part of its training signal is arguably using natural language as a source of supervision.

[P09-S00660] PDF pages [25]
This is an admittedly extremely broad area and covers most work in the field of distributional semantics including topic models (Blei et al., 2003), word, sentence, and paragraph vectors (Mikolov et al., 2013; Kiros et al., 2015; Le & Mikolov, 2014), and language models (Bengio et al., 2003).

[P09-S00661] PDF pages [25]
It also includes much of the broader field of NLP that deals with predicting or modeling sequences of natural language in some way.

[P09-S00662] PDF pages [25]
Work in NLP intentionally leveraging natural language supervision in the form of explanations, feedback, instructions, and advice for tasks such as classification (as opposed to the commonly used representation of supervision as a set of arbitrarily encoded discrete category labels) has 9A model could be unfit for use due to inadequate performance or due to the inappropriateness of AI use in the application area itself.

[P09-S00663] PDF pages [26]
Learning Transferable Visual Models From Natural Language Supervision 26 been explored in many creative and advanced ways.

[P09-S00664] PDF pages [26]
Dialog based learning (Weston, 2016; Li et al., 2016; Hancock et al., 2019) develops techniques to learn from interactive natural language feedback in dialog.

[P09-S00665] PDF pages [26]
Several papers have leveraged semantic parsing to convert natural language explanations into features (Srivastava et al., 2017) or additional training labels (Hancock et al., 2018).

[P09-S00666] PDF pages [26]
More recently, ExpBERT (Murty et al., 2020) uses feature representations produced by conditioning a deep contextual language model on natural language explanations and descriptions of relations to improve performance on the task of relation extraction.

[P09-S00667] PDF pages [26]
CLIP is an example of using natural language as a training signal for learning about a domain other than language.

[P09-S00668] PDF pages [26]
In this context, the earliest use of the term natural language supervision that we are aware of is the work of Ramanathan et al. (2013) which showed that natural language descriptions could be used along side other sources of supervision to improve performance on the task of video event understanding.

[P09-S00669] PDF pages [26]
However, as mentioned in the introduction and approach section, methods of leveraging natural language descriptions in computer vision well predate the use of this specific term, especially for image retrieval (Mori et al., 1999) and object classification (Wang et al., 2009).

[P09-S00670] PDF pages [26]
Other early work leveraged tags (but not natural language) associated with images for the task of semantic segmentation (Barnard et al., 2003).

[P09-S00671] PDF pages [26]
More recently, He & Peng (2017) and Liang et al. (2020) demonstrated using natural language descriptions and explanations to improve fine-grained visual classification of birds.

[P09-S00672] PDF pages [26]
Others have investigated how grounded language can be used to improve visual representations and classifiers on the ShapeWorld dataset (Kuhnle & Copestake, 2017; Andreas et al., 2017; Mu et al., 2019).

[P09-S00673] PDF pages [26]
Finally, techniques which combine natural language with reinforcement learning environments (Narasimhan et al., 2015) have demonstrated exciting emergent behaviors such as systematically accomplishing zero-shot tasks (Hill et al., 2019).

[P09-S00674] PDF pages [26]
CLIP's pre-training task optimizes for text-image retrieval.

[P09-S00675] PDF pages [26]
This areas of research dates back to the mid-90s with the previously mentioned Mori et al. (1999) as representative of early work.

[P09-S00676] PDF pages [26]
While initial efforts focused primarily on predictive objectives over time research shifted towards learning joint multi-modal embedding spaces with techniques like kernel Canonical Correlation Analysis and various ranking objectives (Weston et al., 2010; Socher & Fei-Fei, 2010; Hodosh et al., 2013).

[P09-S00677] PDF pages [26]
Over time work explored many combinations of training objective, transfer, and more expressive models and steadily improved performance (Frome et al., 2013; Socher et al., 2014; Karpathy et al., 2014; Kiros et al., 2014; Faghri et al., 2017).

[P09-S00678] PDF pages [26]
Other work has leveraged natural language supervision for domains other than images.

[P09-S00679] PDF pages [26]
Stroud et al. (2020) explores large scale representation learning by training a system to pair descriptive text with videos instead of images.

[P09-S00680] PDF pages [26]
Several works have explored using dense spoken natural language supervision for videos (Miech et al., 2019; 2020b).

[P09-S00681] PDF pages [26]
When considered together with CLIP, these works suggest that large scale natural language supervision is a promising way to learn high quality perceptual systems for many domains.

[P09-S00682] PDF pages [26]
Alayrac et al. (2020) extended this line of work to an additional modality by adding raw audio as an additional supervision source and demonstrated benefits from combining all three sources of supervision.

[P09-S00683] PDF pages [26]
As part of our work on CLIP we also construct a new dataset of image-text pairs.

[P09-S00684] PDF pages [26]
Modern work on image-text retrieval has relied on a set of crowd-sourced sentence level image caption evaluation datasets like Pascal1K (Rashtchian et al., 2010), Flickr8K (Hodosh et al., 2013), and Flickr30K (Young et al., 2014).

[P09-S00685] PDF pages [26]
However, these datasets are still relatively small and limit achievable performance.

[P09-S00686] PDF pages [26]
Several methods have been proposed to create larger datasets automatically with Ordonez et al. (2011) as a notable early example.

[P09-S00687] PDF pages [26]
In the deep learning era, Mithun et al. (2018) demonstrated an additional set of (image, text) pairs collected from the internet could improve retrieval performance and several new automatically constructed datasets such as Conceptual Captions (Sharma et al., 2018), LAIT (Qi et al., 2020), and OCR-CC (Yang et al., 2020) have been created.

[P09-S00688] PDF pages [26]
However, these datasets still use significantly more aggressive filtering or are designed for a specific task such as OCR and as a result are still much smaller than WIT with between 1 and 10 million training examples.

[P09-S00689] PDF pages [26]
A related idea to CLIP is webly supervised learning.

[P09-S00690] PDF pages [26]
This line of work queries image search engines to build image datasets by querying for terms and uses the queries as the labels for the returned images (Fergus et al., 2005).

[P09-S00691] PDF pages [26]
Classifiers trained on these large but noisily labeled datasets can be competitive with those trained on smaller carefully labeled datasets.

[P09-S00692] PDF pages [26]
These image-query pairs are also often used to improve performance on standard datasets as additional training data (Chen & Gupta, 2015).

[P09-S00693] PDF pages [26]
CLIP also uses search queries as part of its dataset creation process.

[P09-S00694] PDF pages [26]
However CLIP only uses full text sequences co-occuring with images as supervision rather than just the queries, which are often only a single word or short n-gram.

[P09-S00695] PDF pages [26]
We also restrict this step in CLIP to text only querying for sub-string matches while most webly supervised work uses standard image search engines which have their own complex retrieval and filtering pipelines that often involve computer vision systems.

[P09-S00696] PDF pages [26]
Of this line of work, Learning Everything about Anything: Webly-Supervised Visual Concept Learning (Divvala et al., 2014) has a notably similar ambition and goal as CLIP.

[P09-S00697] PDF pages [26, 27]
Finally, CLIP is related to a recent burst of activity on learning joint models of vision and language (Lu et al., 2019; Tan Learning Transferable Visual Models From Natural Language Supervision 27 & Bansal, 2019; Chen et al., 2019; Li et al., 2020b; Yu et al., 2020).

[P09-S00698] PDF pages [27]
This line of work focuses on richly connecting vision and language in order to solve complex downstream tasks such as visual question answering, visual commonsense reasoning, or multimodal entailment.

[P09-S00699] PDF pages [27]
These approaches leverage impressively engineered models which combine 3 (or more) pre-trained subsystems, typically an image feature model, a region proposal / object detection model, and a pre-trained masked language model such as BERT.

[P09-S00700] PDF pages [27]
These systems are then jointly fine-tuned via various training objectives on image-text pairs and applied to the aforementioned tasks and achieve impressive results.

[P09-S00701] PDF pages [27]
CLIP is instead focused on learning visual models from scratch via natural language supervision and does not densely connect the two domains with a joint attention model.

[P09-S00702] PDF pages [27]
The only interaction in a CLIP model between the image and text domain is a single dot product in a learned joint embedding space.

[P09-S00703] PDF pages [27]
We are excited to see CLIP hybridized with this line of work.

[P09-S00704] PDF pages [27]
We have investigated whether it is possible to transfer the success of task-agnostic web-scale pre-training in NLP to another domain.

[P09-S00705] PDF pages [27]
We find that adopting this formula re-sults in similar behaviors emerging in the field of computer vision and discuss the social implications of this line of research.

[P09-S00706] PDF pages [27]
In order to optimize their training objective, CLIP models learn to perform a wide variety of tasks during pre-training.

[P09-S00707] PDF pages [27]
This task learning can then be leveraged via natural language prompting to enable zero-shot transfer to many existing datasets.

[P09-S00708] PDF pages [27]
At sufficient scale, the performance of this approach can be competitive with task-specific supervised models although there is still room for much improvement.

Zhai, X., Puigcerver, J., Kolesnikov, A., Ruyssen, P., Riquelme, C., Lucic, M., Djolonga, J., Pinto, A. S., Neumann, M., Dosovitskiy, A., et al. A large-scale study of representation learning with the visual task adaptation benchmark.

[P09-S00985] PDF pages [36]

[P09-S00986] PDF pages [36]
Zuboff, S. Big other: surveillance capitalism and the prospects of an information civilization.

[P09-S00987] PDF pages [37]
We provide additional details for linear probe experiments presented in this paper, including the list of the datasets and models used for evaluation.

[P09-S00988] PDF pages [37]
We use the 12 datasets from the well-studied evaluation suite introduced by (Kornblith et al., 2019) and add 15 additional datasets in order to assess the performance of models on a wider variety of distributions and tasks.

[P09-S00989] PDF pages [37]
These datasets include MNIST, the Facial Expression Recognition 2013 dataset (Goodfellow et al., 2015), STL-10 (Coates et al., 2011), EuroSAT (Helber et al., 2019), the NWPU- RESISC45 dataset (Cheng et al., 2017), the German Traffic Sign Recognition Benchmark (GTSRB) dataset (Stallkamp et al., 2011), the KITTI dataset (Geiger et al., 2012), PatchCamelyon (Veeling et al., 2018), the UCF101 action recognition dataset (Soomro et al., 2012), Kinetics 700 (Carreira et al., 2019), 2,500 random samples of the CLEVR dataset (Johnson et al., 2017), the Hateful Memes dataset (Kiela et al., 2020), and the ImageNet-1k dataset (Deng et al., 2012).

[P09-S00990] PDF pages [37]
For the two video datasets (UCF101 and Kinetics700), we use the middle frame of each video clip as the input image.

[P09-S00991] PDF pages [37]
STL-10 and UCF101 have multiple pre-defined train/validation/test splits, 10 and 3 respectively, and we report the average over all splits.

[P09-S00992] PDF pages [37]
Details on each dataset and the corresponding evaluation metrics are provided in Table 9.

[P09-S00993] PDF pages [37]
Additionally, we created two datasets that we call Country211 and Rendered SST2.

[P09-S00994] PDF pages [37]
The Country211 dataset is designed to assess the geolocation capability of visual representations.

[P09-S00995] PDF pages [37]
We filtered the YFCC100m dataset (Thomee et al., 2016) to find 211 countries (defined as having an ISO-3166 country code) that have at least 300 photos with GPS coordinates, and we built a balanced dataset with 211 categories, by sampling 200 photos for training and 100 photos for testing, for each country.

[P09-S00996] PDF pages [37]
The Rendered SST2 dataset is designed to measure the optical character recognition capability of visual representations.

[P09-S00997] PDF pages [37]
To do so, we used the sentences from the Stanford Sentiment Treebank dataset (Socher et al., 2013) and rendered them into images, with black texts on a white background, in a 448×448 resolution.

[P09-S00998] PDF pages [37]
Two example images from this dataset are shown in Figure 19.

[P09-S00999] PDF pages [37]
In combination with the datasets listed above, we evaluate the following series of models using linear probes.

[P09-S01000] PDF pages [37]
LM RN50 This is a multimodal model that uses an autoregressive loss instead of a contrastive loss, while using the ResNet-50 architecture as in the smallest contrastive model.

[P09-S01001] PDF pages [37]
To do so, the output from the CNN is projected into four tokens, which are then fed as a prefix to a language model autoregressively predicting the text tokens.

[P09-S01002] PDF pages [37]
Apart from the training objective, the model was trained on the same dataset for the same number of epochs as other CLIP models.

[P09-S01003] PDF pages [37]
CLIP-RN Five ResNet-based contrastive CLIP models are included.

[P09-S01004] PDF pages [37]
As discussed in the paper, the first two models follow ResNet-50 and ResNet-101, and we use EfficientNetstyle (Tan & Le, 2019) scaling for the next three models which simultaneously scale the model width, the number of layers, and the input resolution to obtain models with roughly 4x, 16x, and 64x computation.

[P09-S01005] PDF pages [37]
CLIP-ViT We include four CLIP models that use the Vision Transformer (Dosovitskiy et al., 2020) architecture as the image encoder.

[P09-S01006] PDF pages [37]
We include three models trained on 224- by-224 pixel images: ViT-B/32, ViT-B/16, ViT-L/14, and the ViT-L/14 model fine-tuned on 336-by-336 pixel input images.

[P09-S01007] PDF pages [37]
The largest models (L2-475 and L2-800) take the input resolutions of 475x475 and 800x800 pixels, respectively.

[P09-S01008] PDF pages [37]
Instagram-pretrained ResNeXt We use the four models (32x8d, 32x16d, 32x32d, 32x48d) released by (Mahajan et al., 2018), as well as their two FixRes variants which use higher input resolutions (Touvron et al., 2019).

[P09-S01009] PDF pages [37]
Big Transfer (BiT) We use BiT-S and BiT-M models (Kolesnikov et al., 2019), trained on the ImageNet-1k and ImageNet-21k datasets.

[P09-S01010] PDF pages [37]
The model weights for BiT-L is not publicly available.

[P09-S01011] PDF pages [37]
Vision Transformer (ViT) We also include four ViT (Dosovitskiy et al., 2020) checkpoints pretrained on the ImageNet-21k dataset, namely ViT-B/32, ViT-B/16, ViT- L/16, and ViT-H/14.

[P09-S01012] PDF pages [37]
We note that their best-performing models, trained on the JFT-300M dataset, are not available publicly.

[P09-S01013] PDF pages [37]
SimCLRv2 The SimCLRv2 (Chen et al., 2020c) project released pre-trained and fine-tuned models in various settings.

[P09-S01014] PDF pages [37]
We use the seven pretrain-only checkpoints with selective kernels.

[P09-S01015] PDF pages [37, 38]
BYOL We use the recently released model weights of BYOL (Grill et al., 2020), specifically their 50x1 and 200x2 Learning Transferable Visual Models From Natural Language Supervision 38 Figure 19.

[P09-S01016] PDF pages [38]
Momentum Contrast (MoCo) We include the MoCo-v1 (He et al., 2020) and the MoCo-v2 (Chen et al., 2020d) checkpoints.

[P09-S01017] PDF pages [38]
VirTex We use the pretrained model of VirTex (Desai & Johnson, 2020).

[P09-S01018] PDF pages [38]
We note that VirTex has a similar model design to CLIP-AR but is trained on a 1000x smaller dataset of high-quality captions from MSCOCO.

[P09-S01019] PDF pages [38]
ResNet We add the original ResNet checkpoints released by (He et al., 2016b), namely ResNet-50, ResNet-101, and ResNet152.

[P09-S01020] PDF pages [38]
We use image features taken from the penultimate layer of each model, ignoring any classification layer provided.

[P09-S01021] PDF pages [38]
For CLIP-ViT models, we used the features before the linear projection to the embedding space, which corresponds to I f in Figure 3.

[P09-S01022] PDF pages [38]
We train a logistic regression classifier using scikit-learn's L-BFGS implementation, with maximum 1,000 iterations, and report the corresponding metric for each dataset.

[P09-S01023] PDF pages [38]
We determine the L2 regularization strength λ using a hyperparameter sweep on the validation sets over the range between 10-6 and 106, with 96 logarithmically spaced steps.

[P09-S01024] PDF pages [38]
To save compute required for the sweeps, we perform a parametric binary search that starts with λ = [10-6, 10-4, 10-2, 1, 102, 104, 106] and iteratively halves the interval around the peak until it reaches a resolution of 8 steps per decade.

[P09-S01025] PDF pages [38]
The hyperparameter sweeps are performed on a validation split of each dataset.

[P09-S01026] PDF pages [38]
For the datasets that contain a validation split in addition to a test split, we use the provided validation set to perform the hyperparameter search, and for the datasets that do not provide a validation split or have not published labels for the test data, we split the training dataset to perform the hyperparameter search.

[P09-S01027] PDF pages [38]
For the final result, we combine the validation split back with the training split and report the performance on the unused split.

[P09-S01028] PDF pages [38]
The individual linear probe scores are provided in Table 10 and plotted in Figure 20.

[P09-S01029] PDF pages [38]
The best-performing CLIP model, using ViT-L/14 archiecture and 336-by-336 pixel images, achieved the state of the art in 21 of the 27 datasets, i.e. included in the Clopper-Pearson 99.5% confidence interval around each dataset's top score.

[P09-S01030] PDF pages [38]
For many datasets, CLIP performs significantly better than other models, demonstrating the advantage of natural language supervision over traditional pre-training approaches based on image classification.

[P09-S01031] PDF pages [38]
See Section 3.2 for more discussions on the linear probe results.

[P09-S01032] PDF pages [39]
We note that, for the Birdsnap and Kinetics700 datasets, we used the resources that are available online at the time of this writing.

[P09-S01033] PDF pages [40]
Linear probe performance of various pre-trained models over 27 datasets.

[P09-S01034] PDF pages [40]
Scores within the 99.5% Clopper-Pearson confidence interval of each dataset's top score are shown in bold.

[P09-S01035] PDF pages [41]
Linear probe performance plotted for each of the 27 datasets, using the data from Table 10.

[P09-S01036] PDF pages [42]
Visualization of predictions from 36 CLIP zero-shot classifiers.

[P09-S01037] PDF pages [42]
All examples are random with the exception of reselecting Hateful Memes to avoid offensive content.

[P09-S01038] PDF pages [42]
The predicted probability of the top 5 classes is shown along with the text used to represent the class.

[P09-S01039] PDF pages [42]
When more than one template is used, the first template is shown.

[P09-S01040] PDF pages [42]
The ground truth label is colored green while an incorrect prediction is colored orange.

[P09-S01041] PDF pages [43]
Zero-shot performance of CLIP models over 27 datasets.

[P09-S01042] PDF pages [44]
To provide a qualitative summary / overview of CLIP's zero-shot performance we visualize a randomly selected prediction for 36 different zero-shot CLIP classifiers in Figure 21.

[P09-S01043] PDF pages [44]
In addition, Table 11 and Figure 22 show the individual zero-shot performance scores for each dataset.

[P09-S01044] PDF pages [44]
Our early attempts at duplicate detection and analysis used nearest neighbors in the model's learned embedding space.

[P09-S01045] PDF pages [44]
While it is intuitive to use a model's own notion of similarity, we encountered issues.

[P09-S01046] PDF pages [44]
We found the model's feature space is weighted very heavily towards semantic similarity.

[P09-S01047] PDF pages [44]
We also observed the model was quite poor at assigning certain kinds of near-duplicates high similarity scores.

[P09-S01048] PDF pages [44]
We noticed repeatedly that images with high-frequency textures (such as fur or stripe patterns) pre-processed by different resizing algorithms (nearest neighbor vs bi-linear) could have surprisingly low similarity.

[P09-S01049] PDF pages [44]
This resulted in many false negatives.

[P09-S01050] PDF pages [44]
We built our own near-duplicate detector to fix this issue.

[P09-S01051] PDF pages [44]
We created a synthetic data augmentation pipeline that combined a variety of common image manipulations.

[P09-S01052] PDF pages [44]
The augmentation pipeline combines random cropping and zooming, aspect ratio distortion, downsizing and upscaling to different resolutions, minor rotations, jpeg compression, and HSV color jitter.

[P09-S01053] PDF pages [44]
The pipeline also randomly selects from different interpolation algorithms for all relevant steps.

[P09-S01054] PDF pages [44]
We then trained a model to maximize the similarity of an image and its transformed variant while minimizing similarity to all other images in a training batch.

[P09-S01055] PDF pages [44]
We used the same n-pair / InfoNCE loss as CLIP but with a fixed temperature of 0.07.

[P09-S01056] PDF pages [44]
We selected a ResNet-50 as the model architecture.

[P09-S01057] PDF pages [44]
We modified the base ResNet-50 with the anti-alias improvements from (Zhang, 2019) and used weight norm (Salimans & Kingma, 2016) instead of batch norm (Ioffe & Szegedy, 2015) to avoid leaking information about duplicates via batch statistics - a problem previously noted in (Henaff, 2020).

[P09-S01058] PDF pages [44]
We also found the GELU activation function (Hendrycks & Gimpel, 2016) to perform better for this task.

[P09-S01059] PDF pages [44]
We trained the model with a total batch size of 1,712 for approximately 30 million images sampled from our pre-training dataset.

[P09-S01060] PDF pages [44]
At the end of training it achieves nearly 100% accuracy on its proxy training task.

[P09-S01061] PDF pages [44]
CLIP performs similarly when trained on only YFCC100M.

[P09-S01062] PDF pages [44]
Comparing a ResNet-50 trained on only YFCC100M with a same sized subset of WIT shows simi- lar average performance and number of wins on zero shot and linear classifier evals.

[P09-S01063] PDF pages [44]
However, large differences in dataset specific performance occur.

[P09-S01064] PDF pages [44]
We include performance on the 3 datasets where YFCC does best and worst compared to WIT according to a linear probe in order to highlight this as well as aggregate performance across all linear and zero-shot evals and the canonical ImageNet dataset.

[P09-S01065] PDF pages [44]
To study whether our custom dataset is critical to the performance of CLIP, we trained a model on a filtered subset of the YFCC100M dataset (details described in Section 2.2) and compared its performance to the same model trained on an equally sized subset of WIT.

[P09-S01066] PDF pages [44]
We train each model for 32 epochs at which point transfer performance begins to plateau due to overfitting.

[P09-S01067] PDF pages [44]
Across our whole eval suite, YFCC and WIT perform similarly on average for both zero-shot and linear probe settings.

[P09-S01068] PDF pages [44]
However, performance on specific fine-grained classification datasets can vary widely - sometimes by over 10%.

[P09-S01069] PDF pages [44]
Our speculation is that these differences in performance re-flect the relative density of relevant data in each pre-training dataset.

[P09-S01070] PDF pages [44]
For instance, pre-training on YFCC100M, which might contain many photos of birds and flowers (common subjects for photographers), results in better performance on Birdsnap and Flowers102, while pre-training on WIT results in better car and pet classifiers (which appear common in our dataset).

[P09-S01071] PDF pages [44]
Overall, these results are encouraging as they suggest our approach can use any reasonably filtered collection of paired (text, image) data.

[P09-S01072] PDF pages [44]
This mirrors recent work which reported positive results using the same contrastive pre-training objective on the relatively different domain of medical imaging (Zhang et al., 2020).

[P09-S01073] PDF pages [44]
It also is similar to the findings of noisy student self-training which reported only slight improvements when using their JFT300M dataset over YFCC100M (Xie et al., 2020).

[P09-S01074] PDF pages [44]
We suspect the major advantage of our dataset over the already existing YFCC100M is its much larger size.

[P09-S01075] PDF pages [45]
Learning Transferable Visual Models From Natural Language Supervision 45 Finally, we caution that WIT includes this filtered subset of YFCC100M.

[P09-S01076] PDF pages [45]
This could result in our ablation underestimating the size of performance differences between YFCC100M and the rest of WIT.

[P09-S01077] PDF pages [45]
We do not think this is likely as YFCC100M is only 3.7% of the overall WIT data blend and it did not noticeably change the performance of models when it was added to the existing data blend during the creation of WIT.

[P09-S01078] PDF pages [45]
E. Selected Task and Dataset Results Due to the large variety of datasets and experiments considered in this work, the main body focuses on summarizing and analyzing overall results.

[P09-S01079] PDF pages [45]
In the following subsections we report details of performance for specific groups of tasks, datasets, and evaluation settings.

[P09-S01080] PDF pages [45]
CLIP pre-trains for the task of image-text retrieval on our noisy web-scale dataset.

[P09-S01081] PDF pages [45]
Although the focus of this paper is on representation learning and task learning for the purpose of transfer to a wide variety of downstream datasets, validating that CLIP is able to achieve high transfer performance transfer on exactly what it is pre-trained for is an important sanity check / proof of concept.

[P09-S01082] PDF pages [45]
In Table 13 we check the zero-shot transfer performance of CLIP for both text and image retrieval on the Flickr30k and MSCOCO datsets.

[P09-S01083] PDF pages [45]
Zero-shot CLIP matches or outperforms all prior zero-shot results on these two datasets.

[P09-S01084] PDF pages [45]
Zero-shot CLIP is also competitive with the current overall SOTA for the task of text retrieval on Flickr30k.

[P09-S01085] PDF pages [45]
On image retrieval, CLIP's performance relative to the overall state of the art is noticeably lower.

[P09-S01086] PDF pages [45]
However, zero-shot CLIP is still competitive with a fine-tuned Unicoder-VL.

[P09-S01087] PDF pages [45]
On the larger MS-COCO dataset fine-tuning improves performance significantly and zero-shot CLIP is not competitive with the most recent work.

[P09-S01088] PDF pages [45]
For both these datasets we prepend the prompt “a photo of” to the description of each image which we found boosts CLIP's zero-shot R@1 performance between 1 and 2 points.

[P09-S01089] PDF pages [45]
Although visualizations have shown that ImageNet models contain features that respond to the presence of text in an image (Zeiler & Fergus, 2014), these representations are not sufficiently fine-grained to use for the task of optical character recognition (OCR).

[P09-S01090] PDF pages [45]
To compensate, models are augmented with the outputs of custom OCR engines and features to boost performance on tasks where this capability is required (Singh et al., 2019; Yang et al., 2020).

[P09-S01091] PDF pages [45]
Early during the development of CLIP, we noticed that CLIP began to learn primitive OCR capabilities which appeared to steadily improve over the course of the project.

[P09-S01092] PDF pages [45]
To evaluate this qualitatively noticed behavior, we measured performance on 5 datasets requiring the direct and indirect use of OCR.

[P09-S01093] PDF pages [45]
Three of these datasets MNIST (LeCun), SVHN (Netzer et al., 2011), and IIIT5K (Mishra et al., 2012) directly check the ability of a model to perform low-level character and word recognition, while Hateful Memes (Kiela et al., 2020) and SST-2 (Socher et al., 2013) check the ability of a model to use OCR to perform a semantic task.

[P09-S01094] PDF pages [45]
CLIP's performance is still highly variable and appears to be sensitive to some combination of the domain (rendered or natural images) and the type of text to be recognized (numbers or words).

[P09-S01095] PDF pages [45]
CLIP's OCR performance is strongest Hateful Memes and SST-2 - datasets where the text is digitally rendered and consists mostly of words.

[P09-S01096] PDF pages [45]
On IIIT5K, which is natural images of individually cropped words, zero-shot CLIP performs a bit more respectively and its performance is similar to Jaderberg et al. (2014) early work combining deep learning and structured prediction to perform open-vocabulary OCR.

[P09-S01097] PDF pages [45]
However, performance is noticeably lower on two datasets involving recognition of hand written and street view numbers.

[P09-S01098] PDF pages [45]
CLIP's 51% accuracy on full number SVHN is well below any published results.

[P09-S01099] PDF pages [45]
Inspection suggests CLIP struggles with repeated characters as well as the low resolution and blurry images of SVHN.

[P09-S01100] PDF pages [45]
CLIP's zero-shot MNIST performance is also poor and is outperformed by supervised logistic regression on raw pixels, one of the simplest possible machine learning baselines.

[P09-S01101] PDF pages [45]
SST-2 is a sentence level NLP dataset which we render into images.

[P09-S01102] PDF pages [45]
We include SST-2 in order to check whether CLIP is able to convert low level OCR capability into a higher level representation.

[P09-S01103] PDF pages [45]
Fitting a linear classifier on CLIP's representation of rendered sentences achives 80.5% accuracy.

[P09-S01104] PDF pages [45]
This is on par with the 80% accuracy of a continuous bag of words baseline using GloVe word vectors pre-trained on 840 billion tokens (Pennington et al., 2014).

[P09-S01105] PDF pages [45]
While this is a simple NLP baseline by today's standard, and well below the 97.5% of the current SOTA, it is encouraging to see that CLIP is able to turn an image of rendered text into a non-trivial sentence level representation.

[P09-S01106] PDF pages [45]
Fully supervised CLIP is also surprisingly strong on Hateful Meme detection, where CLIP is only 0.7 points behind the current single model SOTA and several points above the best baseline from the original paper.

[P09-S01107] PDF pages [45]
Similar to SST-2, these other results on Hateful Memes use the ground truth text which CLIP does not have access to.

[P09-S01108] PDF pages [45]
Finally, we note that zero-shot CLIP outperforms the best results using fully supervised linear probes across all other 56 models included in our evaluation suite.

[P09-S01109] PDF pages [45]
This suggests CLIP's OCR capability is at least somewhat unique compared to existing work on self-supervised and supervised representation learning.

[P09-S01110] PDF pages [46]
CLIP improves zero-shot retrieval and is competitive with the best fine-tuned result on Flickr30k text retrieval.

[P09-S01111] PDF pages [46]
Bold indicates best overall performance while an underline indicates best in category performance (zero-shot or fine-tuned).

[P09-S01112] PDF pages [46]
For all other models, best results from the paper are reported regardless of model size / variant.

[P09-S01113] PDF pages [46]
MSCOCO performance is reported on the 5k test set.

[P09-S01114] PDF pages [46]
All metrics are accuracy on the test set except for Hateful Memes which reports ROC AUC on the dev set.

[P09-S01115] PDF pages [46]
Single model SOTA reported to best of knowledge.

[P09-S01116] PDF pages [46]
ES Best reports the best performance across the 56 non-CLIP models in our evaluation suite.

[P09-S01117] PDF pages [46]
For the purpose of learning, a potentially important aspect of natural language is its ability to express, and therefore supervise, an extremely wide set of concepts.

[P09-S01118] PDF pages [46]
A CLIP model, since it is trained to pair semi-arbitrary text with images, is likely to receive supervision for a wide range of visual concepts involving both common and proper nouns, verbs, and adjectives.

[P09-S01119] PDF pages [46]
ImageNet-1K, by contrast, only labels common nouns.

[P09-S01120] PDF pages [46]
Does the lack of broader supervision in ImageNet result in weaker transfer of ImageNet models to tasks involving the recognition of visual concepts that are not nouns?

[P09-S01121] PDF pages [46]
Action recognition performance on 3 video datasets.

[P09-S01122] PDF pages [46]
Sin- gle model SOTA reported to best of knowledge.

[P09-S01123] PDF pages [46]
Note that linear CLIP and linear NS ENet-L2 are trained and evaluated on a single frame subsampled version of each dataset and not directly comparable to prior work.

[P09-S01124] PDF pages [46]
On Kinetics-700, we report the ActivityNet competition metric which is the average of top-1 and top-5 performance.

[P09-S01125] PDF pages [46]
In Table 15 we report results on UCF-101 (Soomro et al., 2012) and Kinetics-700 (Carreira et al., 2019), two common datasets for the task.

[P09-S01126] PDF pages [46]
Unfortunately, our CPU based linear classifier takes a prohibitively long time to evaluate on a video dataset due to the very large number of training frames.

[P09-S01127] PDF pages [46]
To deal with this, we aggressively sub-sample each video to only a single center frame, effectively turning it into an image classification dataset.

[P09-S01128] PDF pages [46]
As a result, our reported performance in a linear evaluation setting likely under estimates performance by a moderate amount.

[P09-S01129] PDF pages [47]
IN is used to abbreviate for ImageNet.

[P09-S01130] PDF pages [47]
CLIP matches the best prior result on UCF- 101 in a linear probe evaluation setting and also outperforms all other models in our evaluation suite.

[P09-S01131] PDF pages [47]
On Kinetics-700, CLIP also outperforms the fine-tuned I3D baseline from the original paper.

[P09-S01132] PDF pages [47]
Since it does not require a training stage, we report CLIP's zero-shot performance when averaging predictions across all frames.

[P09-S01133] PDF pages [47]
CLIP also performs well in this setting and on Kinetics-700 its performance is within 1% of the fully supervised I3D baseline which is trained on 545000 labeled videos.

[P09-S01134] PDF pages [47]
Encouraged by these results, we also measure CLIP's performance on the recently introduced RareAct dataset (Miech et al., 2020a) which was designed to measure zero-shot recognition of unusual actions like “hammering a phone” and “drilling an egg”.

[P09-S01135] PDF pages [47]
CLIP improves over the prior state of the art, a S3D model trained on automatically extracted captions from 100 million instructional videos, by 10 points.

[P09-S01136] PDF pages [47]
While CLIP has encouragingly strong performance on the task of action recognition, we note that there are many differences between the models being compared beyond just their form of supervision such as model architecture, training data distribution, dataset size, and compute used.

[P09-S01137] PDF pages [47]
Further work is needed to more precisely determine what specific design decisions contribute to achieving high performance on this task.

[P09-S01138] PDF pages [47]
Geolocalization performance on the IM2GPS test set.

[P09-S01139] PDF pages [47]
Metric is percent of images localized within a given radius.

[P09-S01140] PDF pages [47]
Models are ordered by average performance.

[P09-S01141] PDF pages [47]
Another behavior we noticed during the development of CLIP was its ability to recognize many places and locations.

[P09-S01142] PDF pages [47]
To quantify this we created the Country211 dataset as described in Appendix A and report results on it throughout the paper.

[P09-S01143] PDF pages [47]
However it is a new benchmark so to compare with prior work on geolocalization we also report results on the IM2GPS test set from Hays & Efros (2008) in Table 17.

[P09-S01144] PDF pages [47]
Since IM2GPS is a regression benchmark, we guess the GPS coordinates of the nearest image in a set of reference images using CLIP's embedding space.

[P09-S01145] PDF pages [47]
This is not a zero-shot result since it uses nearest-neighbor regression.

[P09-S01146] PDF pages [47]
Despite querying only 1 million images, which is much less than prior work, CLIP performs similarly to several task specific models.

[P09-S01147] PDF pages [47]
It is not, however, competitive with the current state of the art.

[P09-S01148] PDF pages [47]
Section 3.3 provides a high level summary and analysis of ImageNet-related robustness results.

[P09-S01149] PDF pages [47]
We briefly provide some additional numerical details in this appendix.

[P09-S01150] PDF pages [47]
Performance results per dataset are provided in Table 16 and compared with the current state of the art results reported in Taori et al. (2020)'s evaluation suite.

[P09-S01151] PDF pages [47]
Zero-shot CLIP improves the state of the art on 5 of the 7 datasets, ImageNet-R, ObjectNet, ImageNet-Sketch, ImageNet-Vid, and Youtube- BB.

[P09-S01152] PDF pages [47]
CLIP's improvements are largest on ImageNet-Vid and Youtube-BB due to its flexible zero-shot capability and on ImageNet-R, which likely reflects CLIP's pre-training distribution including significant amounts of creative content.

[P09-S01153] PDF pages [47]
A similar behavior has been documented for the Instagram pre-trained ResNeXt models as discussed in Taori et al. (2020).
